Follow-up 3: How did you test error handling and edge cases?
Answer: For each component, I tested at least three scenarios: success (data loads correctly), error (API returns error, error message displayed), and empty state (no data, empty state message shown). For forms, I tested validation: submit with empty required fields, invalid email format, boundary values. For the Rule Builder, edge cases included: maximum nested depth, empty condition groups, duplicate conditions, and very long field values. For API errors, I tested 400 (validation error display), 401 (redirect to login), 403 (permission denied message), 404 (not found state), and 500 (generic error with retry). Each error path had at least one test.

Follow-up 4: How did you balance test coverage with development speed?
Answer: Not everything needs the same level of testing. Critical business logic (rule validation, alert processing): high coverage, unit + integration tests. Reusable components (DataTable, FilterBar): thorough unit tests covering all variants and edge cases. Page-level components: integration tests verifying the main user flow. Pure UI components (icons, badges): minimal testing, just snapshot tests. Utility functions: 100% unit test coverage. I never aimed for 100% overall coverage — it's a vanity metric. Instead, I focused on testing the riskiest and most complex areas. A 70% coverage with well-targeted tests is more valuable than 95% with trivial tests.

Follow-up 5: How did you handle flaky tests?
Answer: Flaky tests — tests that pass sometimes and fail sometimes — are dangerous because they erode trust in the test suite. Common causes: timing issues with async operations (fix: use waitFor and findBy queries instead of hard delays), dependency on test execution order (fix: ensure each test is isolated with proper setup/teardown), global state leakage (fix: reset mocks and React Query cache between tests). When a test was identified as flaky, we treated it as a bug — either fixed the root cause or deleted the test. We tracked flaky test frequency and had a 'zero flaky tests' policy.

Follow-up 6: How did E2E tests complement your unit and integration tests?
Answer: E2E tests with Playwright covered critical user journeys that span multiple pages and API interactions: complete login flow, alert dashboard filtering and drill-down, rule creation and verification, and endpoint search and detail view. These caught integration issues that unit tests couldn't — like a navigation link pointing to the wrong route, or a form submission failing due to a CSRF token mismatch. We ran E2E tests on a schedule (nightly) rather than on every PR because they took 5-10 minutes. Failed E2E tests blocked the next day's deployments.

============================================================

Q70: How did you handle documentation for the applications you built?
Answer: Documentation was treated as a deliverable, not an afterthought. We maintained several types: API documentation generated from OpenAPI specs, component documentation in Storybook, architecture documentation in Confluence with diagrams, code-level documentation through TypeScript types and JSDoc comments, and operational documentation — runbooks for deployments, rollbacks, and incident response. The key principle was 'document the why, not the what' — code shows what it does, but documentation explains why it was designed that way. I updated documentation as part of the PR — if a code change affected behavior, the documentation was updated in the same PR.

Follow-up 1: How did you use TypeScript as a form of documentation?
Answer: TypeScript interfaces served as living documentation. When a new developer wanted to understand what data an API returned, they could look at the AlertResponse interface. Prop types on React components documented what each component expected: interface DataTableProps<T> { data: T[]; columns: ColumnDef<T>[]; onRowClick?: (row: T) => void; loading?: boolean; emptyMessage?: string; }. This told developers everything they needed to use the component. Union types like type AlertStatus = 'active' | 'acknowledged' | 'resolved' documented valid values. Unlike written documentation, TypeScript types couldn't become stale — they were enforced by the compiler.

Follow-up 2: How did you document architecture decisions?
Answer: We used Architecture Decision Records (ADRs) in Confluence. Each ADR followed a template: Title (short descriptive name), Date, Status (proposed/accepted/deprecated), Context (what problem we're solving), Options Considered (2-3 alternatives with pros and cons), Decision (what we chose and why), Consequences (trade-offs and implications). Example: 'ADR-007: Use React Query for server state management' documented why we chose React Query over Redux Toolkit Query and SWR. ADRs were reviewed in architecture discussions and referenced in code comments. New team members read ADRs during onboarding.

Follow-up 3: How did you keep documentation up to date?
Answer: The biggest challenge with documentation is staleness. Our strategies: 1) Documentation updates were part of the Definition of Done for stories. 2) Storybook was auto-deployed and linked to Slack — if someone updated a component without updating stories, it was caught in review. 3) Generated documentation (API specs, TypeScript types) was always current by definition. 4) Quarterly documentation reviews — we'd assign each team member a section of Confluence to review and update. 5) 'Doc debt' was tracked alongside tech debt. When a developer found outdated documentation, they filed a JIRA ticket and ideally fixed it immediately.

Follow-up 4: What makes good code-level documentation?
Answer: Good code documentation explains intent, not mechanics. Bad: // loop through alerts. Good: // Deduplicate alerts by ID, keeping the most recent version. Alerts can arrive through both subscription and polling, resulting in duplicates during high-traffic periods. I documented: why a function exists (not what it does — the code shows that), assumptions and constraints ('assumes alerts are sorted by timestamp'), edge cases ('returns empty array if no alerts match, never null'), and business rules ('alerts older than 90 days are considered archived'). Complex algorithms got block comments explaining the approach. Simple CRUD code needed no comments.

Follow-up 5: How did you document API integrations for the frontend team?
Answer: Beyond Swagger: we maintained a 'Frontend API Guide' in Confluence that documented each API from the frontend perspective — what data do I send, what do I get back, what errors can occur, and what's the caching strategy. This was more practical than raw Swagger because it included: example request/response pairs for common scenarios, error handling recommendations ('on 401, trigger token refresh'), performance notes ('use pagination for endpoints, max page size 100'), and known quirks ('the created_at field uses UTC, convert for display'). This guide was the first place frontend developers looked when starting a new integration.

Follow-up 6: How did you handle documentation for your freelance clients?
Answer: For freelance clients who weren't technical, I provided: a user guide with screenshots explaining how to use the application, an admin guide for content management and configuration, a technical handover document with setup instructions, environment variables, deployment steps, and a brief architecture overview. For clients who might hire other developers later, I included: codebase structure explanation, technology choices and reasons, third-party service accounts and configurations, and known limitations. Good documentation was a differentiator — clients appreciated it, and it reduced post-delivery support requests. Some of my documentation templates carried over to enterprise documentation practices.

============================================================

Q71: How do you approach continuous learning and staying current with technology as a software engineer?
Answer: I maintain a structured learning approach. Daily: I skim tech newsletters (This Week in React, JavaScript Weekly) and GitHub trending repositories. Weekly: I deep-dive into one topic — reading documentation, building a small prototype, or studying an open-source library's source code. For example, I studied React Query's source to understand its caching and garbage collection mechanism, which helped me debug caching issues more effectively. Monthly: I try to complete a small side project or contribute to documentation. For certifications, I study systematically — my AWS Developer Associate certification took about 6 weeks of structured preparation with practice exams.

Follow-up 1: How did you prepare for and achieve your AWS certifications?
Answer: For the Cloud Practitioner: 3 weeks, focused on understanding AWS service categories, pricing models, and the shared responsibility model. I used the official training, Stephane Maarek's Udemy course, and practice exams. For the Developer Associate: 6 weeks, deeper study of Lambda, DynamoDB, API Gateway, S3, CloudFormation, and CI/CD on AWS. I built a small serverless application end-to-end as part of my preparation — this hands-on practice was more valuable than memorizing services. Practice exams were critical — they exposed gaps in my understanding and taught me how AWS frames questions. The certifications validated knowledge I was already using at work.

Follow-up 2: How do you evaluate whether to adopt a new technology or library?
Answer: I evaluate on five criteria: 1) Does it solve a real problem we have? (Not 'is it trending?') 2) Community and maintenance — active development, responsive maintainers, good documentation. 3) Migration cost — how much existing code needs to change? 4) Team readiness — can the team learn it within a reasonable timeframe? 5) Risk — what happens if the library is abandoned? Can we fork or replace it? For example, we adopted React Query because it solved real server-state management pain points, had excellent documentation, and the migration could be done incrementally. We passed on a newer alternative that was interesting but had a small community.

Follow-up 3: What technologies are you currently learning or interested in?
Answer: I'm deepening my Java and Spring Boot knowledge to become more effective in full-stack enterprise environments — which aligns directly with EY's tech stack. I'm also exploring Next.js for server-side rendering patterns and understanding when SSR is appropriate versus SPA. On the cloud side, I'm studying AWS solutions architecture patterns — designing for high availability, disaster recovery, and cost optimization. I'm also following the AI/LLM space — understanding how to integrate AI features into enterprise applications, like intelligent alert triage or natural language rule creation.

Follow-up 4: How do you apply what you learn to your current work?
Answer: Every learning exercise has a practical application. When I studied React Query's internals, I immediately applied that knowledge to optimize our caching strategy — changing staleTime and cacheTime based on data freshness requirements. Learning about Web Workers led me to offload CSV export processing. Studying DynamoDB best practices led me to suggest adding a GSI for a common query pattern the backend team hadn't optimized. AWS certification knowledge helped me have informed discussions about infrastructure decisions — I could suggest Lambda provisioned concurrency for latency-sensitive functions, which the team adopted.

Follow-up 5: How do you share your learning with the team?
Answer: I share through multiple channels: Brown bag presentations — I've presented on React Query deep-dive, WebSocket architecture patterns, and AWS Lambda best practices. Code review comments — when I learn a better pattern, I suggest it in reviews with links to documentation. Team wiki — I write short 'TIL (Today I Learned)' articles on Confluence when I discover useful patterns. Pair programming — when implementing something I recently learned, I pair with a teammate so the knowledge transfers naturally. Study groups — for AWS certification preparation, I organized a small study group, and we discussed topics weekly.

Follow-up 6: Where do you see your career heading in the next 2-3 years?
Answer: I want to grow into a senior full-stack engineer capable of leading technical design for enterprise applications. In the short term at EY, I want to deepen my backend skills — working more with Java, Spring Boot, and database design — while leveraging my strong frontend foundation. I want to lead the frontend architecture on a client project and eventually own end-to-end feature design. In 2-3 years, I'd like to be the engineer who can architect a complete application, from React UI to Spring Boot APIs to AWS infrastructure, and mentor junior engineers on the team. The EY model of diverse client projects is ideal for this growth — each project exposes me to different domains and technical challenges.

Q72: Can you walk me through the projects you've worked on?
Answer: Sure. At BluSapphire I worked on two production systems. The first is the EDR OneAgent Platform — this is a full endpoint detection and response product. We have lightweight agents installed on customer machines — laptops, servers — and those agents send security telemetry to our AWS backend. I built the React-based management console where security analysts can see all their endpoints, monitor alerts, manage policies, and investigate threats. The second project is the Rule Builder — a visual interface that lets security teams create and deploy detection rules without writing raw SQL. Both systems are multi-tenant, meaning one deployment serves hundreds of customer organizations, and I worked across the full stack: React and TypeScript on the frontend, GraphQL via AWS AppSync for the API layer, Python Lambdas for backend logic, and AWS services like DynamoDB, S3, Kinesis Firehose, and SNS for infrastructure.

Follow-up 1: Which project did you spend the most time on and why?
Answer: EDR OneAgent took about 70 percent of my time because it is the core product. It touches everything — agent lifecycle, real-time dashboards, investigation queries, policy management. There is always new feature work, performance improvements, or cross-team coordination happening. The Rule Builder was a focused three-month build that I owned end to end, but OneAgent is an ongoing effort with continuous iterations.

Follow-up 2: What was your specific role vs the team's role?
Answer: I was one of three full-stack engineers. I owned the entire frontend console — all the React components, the GraphQL integration with AppSync, and the state management. On the backend side, I worked on specific Lambda handlers for things like agent lifecycle, async investigation queries, and dashboard data. The senior engineers handled infrastructure-as-code with CDK and the data pipeline. I also did code reviews and wrote integration tests for the AppSync resolvers.

Follow-up 3: How did these two projects interact with each other?
Answer: The Rule Builder feeds directly into OneAgent's detection engine. When a security analyst creates a rule in the Rule Builder, it gets stored as a policy configuration in S3 and DynamoDB. Then when an agent on a customer endpoint calls getPolicy, it receives the latest rules that were created through the Rule Builder. So think of the Rule Builder as the authoring tool, and OneAgent as the execution and monitoring layer. They share the same DynamoDB tables for policy mappings and the same S3 bucket for policy storage.

============================================================

Q73: Explain the overall architecture of the EDR OneAgent platform.
Answer: The platform has two main stacks. The first is the Agent Stack — this handles all communication between the agents installed on customer endpoints and our backend. It uses API Gateway with mutual TLS authentication, meaning agents present a client certificate to prove their identity. Behind the API Gateway sits a Python Lambda that processes enrollment, heartbeats, config fetching, and policy retrieval. The second is the Console Stack — this is what the security analysts use. It is built on AWS AppSync, which is a managed GraphQL service. The React frontend talks to AppSync using GraphQL queries and mutations for things like fetching agent lists, viewing alerts, and managing policies. For real-time features like investigation query results, we use AppSync subscriptions — the backend pushes results to the frontend over WebSocket without any polling. On the data side, agents send raw telemetry events directly to Kinesis Data Firehose, which delivers them as gzipped JSON to S3. An external pipeline transforms those into Parquet format in a data lake. For ad-hoc investigation queries, we run DuckDB inside a Lambda — it reads the Parquet files directly from S3, and we use a Dragonfly file index to quickly locate which files to scan.

Follow-up 1: Why two separate stacks instead of one unified API?
Answer: It comes down to security isolation. The agent stack uses mutual TLS — agents authenticate with client certificates, not API keys or JWTs. The console stack uses Stytch B2B session tokens for analyst login. Mixing these two completely different auth models in one API Gateway would create unnecessary complexity and a bigger attack surface. Keeping them separate also lets us scale independently. Agent heartbeat traffic is orders of magnitude higher than console queries, so the agent Lambda needs different concurrency and memory settings.

Follow-up 2: Why AppSync instead of a REST API for the console?
Answer: Three reasons. First, our domain is deeply nested — an agent belongs to a zone, which has policies, which have rules — and GraphQL's type system models that very naturally. Second, AppSync gives us subscriptions out of the box — when an investigation query finishes processing, the result is pushed to the frontend in real time over WebSocket without us managing any WebSocket infrastructure. Third, we use a single Lambda resolver pattern where one Lambda handles all GraphQL operations and routes internally based on the field name. This keeps deployment simple. The trade-off is some cold start latency, but we handle that with provisioned concurrency on the resolver Lambda.

Follow-up 3: How do you handle multi-tenancy across environments?
Answer: Every single request carries organization context. On the agent side, the customerID maps to DynamoDB partition keys like AGENT##{org_slug}. On the console side, when an analyst logs in through Stytch, the authentication returns a resolverContext containing their organization_id, organization_slug, and organization_region. This context gets passed to every Lambda invocation automatically. All DynamoDB queries use the org_slug in the partition key, so tenant isolation is enforced at the data layer — there is no way for one org to accidentally see another org's data.

Follow-up 4: Can you draw or describe the data flow from agent to frontend?
Answer: Sure, let me walk through it step by step. An agent on a customer endpoint does two things continuously. First, it sends heartbeats every 60 seconds through API Gateway to our Lambda, which updates the agent's metadata in DynamoDB — things like last seen time, OS, version, and health. Second, it sends raw security events — process creation, file access, network connections — directly to Kinesis Data Firehose. Firehose batches and compresses these and delivers them to S3 as gzipped JSON files. An external pipeline then transforms those JSON files into Parquet format in our data lake, and updates a Dragonfly file index so we can quickly find which Parquet files contain which data. Now on the console side, the React frontend communicates with AppSync. For regular data like agent lists and policies, it uses GraphQL queries — AppSync routes to our Lambda, which reads from DynamoDB and returns the result. For investigation queries, it is asynchronous — the frontend calls a startEdrAsyncQuery mutation, the Lambda publishes the request to SNS and returns a requestId immediately. The frontend then subscribes to onEdrAsyncQueryResponse using that requestId. A separate worker Lambda picks up the SNS message, runs a DuckDB query over the Parquet files in S3, and publishes results back through AppSync. The frontend receives them in real time through the subscription. No polling needed.

============================================================

Q74: Walk me through what happens when an EDR agent is installed on an endpoint.
Answer: It is a multi-phase process. First, on the console side, an admin enables EDR for their organization by calling a setEdrForOrg mutation. Then they create agent packages through a createAgents mutation — this triggers a Lambda that reads the OS-specific binary from an admin S3 bucket, bundles it with org-specific config files and mutual TLS certificates, creates a ZIP, and uploads it to a customer-specific S3 bucket. The admin then downloads this ZIP from the console using a presigned S3 URL. After running the installer on the endpoint, the agent makes its first API call — POST /enroll with customerID and endpointID. The Lambda checks DynamoDB for a pre-registered record. If found, enrollment succeeds. Next, the agent calls get_config to receive its Kinesis Firehose stream name, IAM Roles Anywhere credentials, and heartbeat interval. From there, it starts periodic heartbeats and policy fetches. So the flow is: admin creates package in console → downloads ZIP → installs on endpoint → agent enrolls → agent starts heartbeating and sending telemetry.

Follow-up 1: Why do you require pre-registration before enrollment?
Answer: It is a security control. We do not want random endpoints enrolling themselves — that would be an attack vector where a compromised machine could register as a legitimate agent. The admin has to first create the agent record through the console using an addAgent call. This establishes a known-good inventory. The enrollment call is essentially a handshake that confirms 'yes, we expected this endpoint.' If the agent is not pre-registered, the Lambda returns an error and the agent will not proceed.

Follow-up 2: How does the certificate-based authentication work?
Answer: When we build the agent package, we include a client certificate and private key generated from our internal certificate authority. When the agent makes any API call to our backend, it presents this certificate during the TLS handshake. API Gateway has a custom authorizer Lambda that extracts the certificate serial number and validates it against our CA. This is mutual TLS — the server verifies the client, not just the other way around. If the certificate is approaching expiry, the heartbeat response includes a 'renew cert' command so the agent can get a fresh certificate.

Follow-up 3: What's in the get_config response and why?
Answer: It returns three important things. First, the Kinesis Data Firehose delivery stream name — the agent sends raw telemetry events directly to Firehose rather than through our REST API, because the event volume is much higher than what a request-response API should handle. Second, IAM Roles Anywhere configuration — the trust anchor ARN, profile ARN, role ARN, and region — so the agent can use its certificate to get temporary AWS credentials for writing to Firehose. And third, the heartbeat interval, typically 60 seconds. The key insight is that the agent does not need long-lived AWS access keys. It uses its certificate to get short-lived IAM credentials through Roles Anywhere, which is much more secure.

Follow-up 4: How does the package build pipeline work with SNS?
Answer: When the createAgents mutation runs, the Lambda publishes a message to an SNS topic. A separate process Lambda is subscribed to this topic. When it receives the message, it reads the base binary from the admin S3 bucket, bundles it with the org-specific config — API endpoint, customer ID — and the certificates from our CA bucket, creates a ZIP, and uploads it to the customer agent bucket. We use SNS to decouple the API response from the slow build process. The API Lambda returns immediately while the build happens in the background — it can take 30 to 60 seconds. The user sees an immediate confirmation rather than waiting. For new version rollouts, a separate Lambda watches an ingest bucket. When a new binary is uploaded, it fans out SNS messages to all orgs so their packages get rebuilt with the latest version.

============================================================

Q75: How does the heartbeat mechanism work and why is it important?
Answer: Every 60 seconds, each agent sends a POST to /doheartbeat with its current state — customerID, agentID, connection status, computer name, local IP, agent version, policy hash, OS details, CPU and memory usage, last reboot time, and domain info. The Lambda fetches the existing agent record from DynamoDB, updates all these fields along with the lastseen timestamp, and then returns any pending commands. The heartbeat serves two purposes. First, it is the single source of truth for agent health on the console — the frontend reads lastseen to show whether an agent is online or offline, and displays version, OS, and resource usage. Second, it is a command channel — if an admin deleted the agent record, the Lambda returns an 'uninstall' command and the agent removes itself from the endpoint.

Follow-up 1: How do you determine if an agent is online or offline?
Answer: We use the lastseen timestamp in DynamoDB. The console applies a threshold — if lastseen is older than about three times the heartbeat interval, roughly three minutes, the agent is marked offline. We chose this approach because we do not maintain persistent connections to agents. The trade-off is a detection delay — if an agent crashes, it will still appear online for up to three minutes. But maintaining WebSocket connections to thousands of agents across hundreds of organizations would be significantly more complex and expensive, so this simple threshold approach works well for our scale.

Follow-up 2: What kind of commands can the heartbeat response include?
Answer: The main ones are: 'renew_cert' when the agent's TLS certificate is approaching expiry, 'uninstall' when the agent record has been deleted from the console, and 'register' for re-registration scenarios. We also use it to push configuration changes — if the agent's policy hash does not match the current policy, the heartbeat response signals it to call getPolicy to fetch the updated rules. This command channel avoids us needing a separate push mechanism. We piggyback on the pull-based heartbeat, which keeps the architecture simple.

Follow-up 3: How do you handle the scale of thousands of agents heartbeating every 60 seconds?
Answer: DynamoDB handles the write throughput natively — each heartbeat is a single UpdateItem operation with the partition key AGENT##{org_slug} and the sort key as endpointID. DynamoDB auto-scales write capacity based on demand. The Lambda is configured with higher concurrency limits for the agent stack. We also designed the heartbeat payload to be self-contained — one DynamoDB write per heartbeat, no secondary lookups — so each invocation finishes fast, typically under 100 milliseconds. If we ever hit Lambda concurrency limits, we would add an SQS buffer in front, but at our current scale of tens of thousands of agents, direct Lambda invocation handles it just fine.

============================================================

Q76: How does event data flow from the agent to the analytics dashboard?
Answer: Let me walk through the full pipeline. The agent on the endpoint generates raw EDR events — process creation, file access, network connections, registry changes — and sends them directly to Kinesis Data Firehose using temporary IAM credentials it got through Roles Anywhere. Firehose buffers these events, compresses them with gzip, and delivers them as files to an S3 raw events bucket, partitioned by date and organization. An external data pipeline then transforms these raw JSON files into Parquet format and writes them to a data lake bucket. At the same time, the pipeline updates a Dragonfly file index — this is a Redis-compatible in-memory store that maps keys like file_index:{org}:{date}:{logtype} to the actual Parquet file paths in S3. Now when an analyst wants to investigate something on the dashboard, the frontend calls a GraphQL query through AppSync. For things like alert counts and MITRE tactic distributions, the Lambda runs analytics queries over this data. For ad-hoc investigation queries — like 'show me all process events from this host in the last week' — we use the async flow: the Lambda publishes to SNS, a worker Lambda picks it up, looks up the relevant Parquet files through the Dragonfly index, runs a DuckDB query directly over those files in S3, and pushes results back to the frontend through an AppSync subscription.

Follow-up 1: Why Kinesis Firehose instead of sending events through the REST API?
Answer: Volume. An active endpoint can generate thousands of events per minute — process starts, file operations, network connections. Sending each one through API Gateway to a Lambda to DynamoDB would be prohibitively expensive and slow. Firehose is designed for high-throughput streaming ingestion — it automatically buffers, batches, compresses, and delivers to S3. The agent writes directly to Firehose using its IAM credentials, completely bypassing our REST API. This cleanly separates the control plane — enrollment, heartbeats, policy fetches, which are low-volume request-response operations — from the data plane — telemetry events, which are high-volume fire-and-forget.

Follow-up 2: Why Parquet format instead of just storing JSON?
Answer: Query performance. Raw JSON in S3 would require reading and parsing entire files for any query. Parquet is a columnar format — when a dashboard query only needs timestamp, severity, and MITRE tactic, it reads only those three columns and skips everything else. This reduces I/O by 80 to 90 percent. Parquet also supports compression per column and stores min/max statistics that allow query engines to skip irrelevant data chunks without even reading them. The combination is what makes our DuckDB queries fast — instead of scanning gigabytes of raw JSON, DuckDB reads only the relevant columns from the relevant Parquet files.

Follow-up 3: What role does Dragonfly play in the query flow?
Answer: Dragonfly is our fast file index. When an analyst runs an investigation query, we need to know which Parquet files in S3 contain the data we need. Without an index, we would have to list the S3 bucket, which is slow when you have millions of files. Instead, we look up a Dragonfly key like file_index:{org}:{date}:{logtype} and instantly get back the exact S3 paths of the Parquet files we need. Then DuckDB reads only those specific files. This two-step approach — fast index lookup, then targeted file read — is what makes our ad-hoc queries return in seconds instead of minutes.

Follow-up 4: How do async queries work end-to-end?
Answer: Here is the full flow. The analyst clicks 'Investigate' on the console and enters their query parameters. The frontend calls the startEdrAsyncQuery GraphQL mutation with the query details. The Lambda publishes this to an SNS topic and immediately returns a requestId — so the user is not blocked. The frontend then subscribes to onEdrAsyncQueryResponse using that requestId through an AppSync WebSocket subscription. Meanwhile, a worker Lambda gets triggered by the SNS message. It looks up the Dragonfly file index to find which Parquet files to scan, runs the DuckDB query over those files in S3, and as it makes progress, it publishes updates back through AppSync using an IAM-signed GraphQL mutation. The frontend receives these updates in real time through its subscription — the user sees a progress bar moving, and then the results appear. No polling, no refreshing. The whole thing typically takes 5 to 30 seconds depending on data volume.

============================================================

Q77: How does the policy and zone system work?
Answer: Zones are logical groupings of agents — for example, you might have a 'Production Servers' zone and a 'Developer Workstations' zone within an organization. Each zone has a policy mapping that points to a policy configuration file stored in S3. When an agent calls getPolicy, the Lambda looks up the agent's zone in DynamoDB, finds the policy mapping for that zone and platform — Windows or Linux — reads the policy JSON from S3, transforms it by adding MITRE tags and other metadata, and returns it to the agent. But here is the important part — it only returns the policy if the hash is different from what the agent already has. This hash-based diffing prevents unnecessary policy downloads over the network. Policies define detection rules, exclusions, response actions, and collection settings that the agent enforces on the endpoint.

Follow-up 1: Why store policies in S3 instead of DynamoDB?
Answer: Size and structure. A policy document can be several hundred kilobytes of structured JSON with deeply nested rule definitions, MITRE mappings, and configuration blocks. DynamoDB has a 400KB item size limit and is not designed for storing large documents. S3 gives us unlimited object size, built-in versioning for rollback, and lower cost for large objects. So DynamoDB stores the metadata — which zone maps to which policy file, the version, last modified timestamp — while S3 stores the actual policy content. This is a very common pattern: metadata in a fast key-value store, blobs in object storage.

Follow-up 2: How does a policy update propagate to all agents?
Answer: When an admin updates a policy through the console, the Lambda writes the new policy JSON to S3 and updates the version hash in DynamoDB. Agents do not get push notifications — instead, during their next getPolicy call, which happens periodically or when a heartbeat response indicates a policy mismatch through the policyHash field, they detect the hash difference and download the new version. For default policy template updates that affect all organizations, an S3 trigger fires a fanout Lambda that publishes SNS messages per org, and a refresh Lambda processes each one to update the policy mappings. The worst-case propagation delay is one heartbeat interval, which is 60 seconds.

Follow-up 3: How do zones enable multi-tenancy?
Answer: Zones are scoped to an organization — the DynamoDB partition key includes the org slug. So an org can create zones like 'Production Servers', 'Developer Workstations', and 'Executive Laptops' with completely different security policies for each group. When an agent is added to a zone via the addAgentsToZone mutation, its DynamoDB record is updated with the new zoneid. This means a single organization can have different detection sensitivity, different exclusion rules, and different response actions for different groups of endpoints. Cross-org zone access is impossible because the resolverContext from Stytch limits all queries to the authenticated org's data partition.

============================================================

Q78: How does authentication work for the console and for agents?
Answer: We have two completely separate authentication mechanisms because the console and the agents have very different trust models. For the console, we use Stytch B2B authentication. When an analyst logs in, the frontend gets a session JWT. This JWT is sent with every AppSync request, and AppSync invokes a Lambda authorizer that validates it with Stytch's API. The authorizer returns a resolverContext containing the user's email, organization_id, organization_slug, organization_region, and organization_type. This context gets passed to every resolver Lambda, ensuring all data access is scoped to the right organization. For agents, we use mutual TLS — the agent presents a client certificate during the TLS handshake with API Gateway. A custom authorizer Lambda extracts the certificate serial number and validates it against our internal certificate authority. No API keys, no JWTs — certificate-based identity is harder to steal than tokens and works well for machines.

Follow-up 1: Why Stytch instead of Cognito or Auth0?
Answer: Stytch's B2B product was the best fit for our multi-tenant SaaS model. It provides organization-level session management out of the box — each user belongs to an organization with its own auth settings, member management, and SSO configuration. Cognito's multi-tenancy requires separate user pools per tenant, which does not scale well operationally. Auth0 could work, but Stytch's API design and B2B primitives like organizations, members, and roles mapped directly to our domain without needing translation layers.

Follow-up 2: How does the resolverContext enable security?
Answer: This is a critical piece of our security model. Every AppSync request goes through the auth Lambda, which injects the resolverContext. This is non-spoofable because it comes from the server-side Lambda, not from the client. The resolver Lambda receives event.identity.resolverContext with the org_slug, org_id, and user email. All DynamoDB queries use the org_slug in the partition key, all S3 paths are org-scoped, and all data queries include an org filter. The user never passes their org_id in the request — it is derived from their authenticated session. This prevents horizontal privilege escalation where a user might try to access another organization's data.

Follow-up 3: What is IAM Roles Anywhere and why use it for agents?
Answer: IAM Roles Anywhere lets workloads running outside of AWS authenticate using X.509 certificates instead of long-lived access keys. Our agents run on customer endpoints — we cannot embed AWS access keys in the installer because they would be extractable from the binary. Instead, the agent uses its client certificate to request temporary IAM credentials through Roles Anywhere. These credentials are scoped to allow only PutRecord on the specific Kinesis Firehose stream — nothing else. The credentials expire in one hour and auto-renew. If a certificate is compromised, we revoke it from our CA and the agent can no longer get credentials. It is the principle of least privilege combined with short-lived credentials.

============================================================

Q79: How does the alerts and dashboard system work?
Answer: The dashboard displays pre-aggregated security metrics: risk distribution by zone, top critical hosts, MITRE ATT&CK tactic overview, detection timeline, and the overall org risk score. When the frontend needs this data, it calls GraphQL queries like getAlertsList, getTopCriticalHosts, or getTacticsOverview through AppSync. The resolver Lambda receives the request, scoped to the authenticated organization through the resolverContext, and queries the data lake. For pre-aggregated analytics, the Lambda constructs SQL and runs it against the processed data. For ad-hoc investigation drilldowns, it uses the async DuckDB flow I described earlier. Alert stats include severity distribution, trend over time, and MITRE technique mapping. The key thing that makes this fast is the Parquet columnar format combined with the Dragonfly file index — the Lambda knows exactly which files to read and which columns to pull, so even queries over large datasets return quickly.

Follow-up 1: What MITRE ATT&CK data do you surface on the dashboard?
Answer: We map every detection to MITRE ATT&CK tactics and techniques. The dashboard has several views. getTacticsOverview shows a heatmap of tactics — Initial Access, Execution, Persistence, and so on — with color intensity based on alert volume. getTopMitreAttCkTTPs shows the most frequently triggered techniques, like T1059.001 PowerShell or T1053.005 Scheduled Task. mitreAttCkTimeline shows how tactic triggers change over time, which helps analysts spot attack campaigns progressing through stages. And mitreTechniquesByTopHosts shows which endpoints trigger which techniques most often. This MITRE mapping happens in the detection pipeline — when events match rules, the rule's MITRE tags are stored alongside the alert. So every alert carries its MITRE context from creation to display.

Follow-up 2: How do you handle loading and error states on the dashboard?
Answer: Each dashboard widget is an independent React component with its own GraphQL query. We use Apollo Client's useQuery hook, which gives us loading, error, and data states. During loading, we show skeleton components that match the expected layout to prevent layout shift. On error, we show a retry button with the error type. For the async investigation flow, we show a progress bar that updates through the AppSync subscription — the worker Lambda publishes percentage progress, so the user sees something like 'Scanning files... 45 percent' rather than just a spinner. This progress feedback was one of my contributions and significantly improved the user experience.

Follow-up 3: How do different regions work for analytics?
Answer: Each production region has its own data lake and processing infrastructure. The resolver Lambda selects the appropriate endpoints based on the organization_region from the resolverContext. This ensures data residency — Mumbai customers' data stays in the Mumbai region. The schema and data formats are consistent across regions, so the Lambda logic is the same — only the endpoint URLs change, and those are configured through environment variables.

============================================================

Q80: How did you design the DynamoDB schema for OneAgent?
Answer: We use a single-table design with composite keys. The main table uses partition keys with entity prefixes — AGENT##{org_slug} for agents with the sort key as endpointID, POLICYMAPPING##{org_slug} for policy mappings with sort key as zoneid, EDRAGENT##{org_slug} for package metadata with sort key as filename. This puts all related data for an organization on the same partition, which makes queries very efficient. We also have separate tables for cross-cutting concerns: a console-customer table for product and license information and an admin-orgs table for organization hierarchy. The key design principle is that every query pattern is satisfied by a single GetItem or Query operation — we never do table scans.

Follow-up 1: Why single-table design over multiple tables?
Answer: It aligns with DynamoDB best practices for access pattern optimization. All our queries are org-scoped — get agents for org X, get policies for org X, get packages for org X. With a single table and org-prefixed partition keys, each of those queries is a single partition read. If we had multiple tables, we would need multiple round-trips to get related data. The trade-off is schema complexity — you need to understand the partition key and sort key patterns — but the benefit is consistent single-digit millisecond latency regardless of how large the table grows.

Follow-up 2: How do you handle the 400KB item size limit?
Answer: By separating metadata from content. DynamoDB items store agent metadata — name, version, OS, lastseen, health status — which is well under 10KB per item. Large objects like policy documents are stored in S3, and the DynamoDB item just contains a reference to the S3 key. For agent event data, we do not store events in DynamoDB at all — those go through Firehose to S3. So DynamoDB is our hot metadata store for fast lookups, and S3 is our content store for large objects.

Follow-up 3: How does cross-region work for Prod3?
Answer: Prod3 serves Mumbai customers. The Lambda functions run in ap-south-1, but some DynamoDB tables live in us-east-1 for centralized management. We use DynamoDB global tables for replication where needed, and for some tables the Lambda simply points directly to the us-east-1 table endpoint. This adds about 50 to 100 milliseconds of cross-region latency, but it ensures a single source of truth. Since heartbeat writes are latency-tolerant — agents retry on failure — and console reads where 100ms is acceptable, this approach works. The alternative of maintaining completely separate tables per region would require complex replication logic.

============================================================

Q81: How is the frontend console built and how does it communicate with the backend?
Answer: The console is a React and TypeScript single-page application. It communicates with our AWS AppSync backend using Apollo Client. All data fetching happens through GraphQL — we have queries for reading data like getAgentsList and getAlertsList, mutations for writing data like addAgent, updatePolicy, and downloadAgent, and subscriptions for real-time updates like onEdrAsyncQueryResponse. Apollo Client manages a normalized cache — when a mutation updates an agent, the cache automatically updates all views showing that agent, so the UI stays consistent without manual refetching. For complex UI state like filter selections and view preferences, we use React context. Authentication tokens from Stytch are attached to every AppSync request through Apollo Link middleware, so the backend always knows which organization the user belongs to.

Follow-up 1: How do you handle loading and error states for dashboard data?
Answer: Each dashboard widget is an independent React component with its own GraphQL query using Apollo's useQuery hook. This gives us loading, error, and data states for each widget independently. During loading, we show skeleton components that match the expected layout to prevent content from jumping around. On error, we show a retry button with the error type — network error versus server error. For the async investigation flow, we use AppSync subscriptions to show a progress bar. The worker Lambda publishes percentage progress, so the user sees real progress like 'Scanning files... 45 percent' instead of just a spinner. This makes waiting for results much less frustrating.

Follow-up 2: How does the download agent flow work from the frontend?
Answer: The user selects an agent package — picking the OS and version — and clicks download. This triggers a downloadAgent GraphQL mutation with the organization_slug and filepath. The resolver Lambda generates a presigned S3 URL pointing to the customer agent bucket — this URL is valid for 15 minutes. The frontend receives the URL and initiates a browser download, either by redirecting window.location.href or by creating an anchor element and clicking it programmatically. The important thing is that the file never passes through Lambda or AppSync — the browser downloads directly from S3. This matters because agent packages can be 50 to 100 megabytes, and passing that through Lambda would be inefficient and hit size limits.

Follow-up 3: How do you handle the agents list with potentially thousands of agents?
Answer: We use pagination at the GraphQL level. The getAgentsList query accepts limit and nextToken parameters. The Lambda queries DynamoDB with a Limit and returns the ExclusiveStartKey as the nextToken for the next page. On the frontend, we implement infinite scroll — when the user scrolls near the bottom, we call Apollo's fetchMore with the nextToken. Apollo's cache automatically merges the new page with existing results. We also support server-side filtering — the query accepts zone, status, and OS filters, so DynamoDB returns only matching items rather than us filtering thousands of agents on the client side.

============================================================

Q82: Why do you use SNS extensively and how do the async patterns work?
Answer: SNS decouples our API responses from expensive background processing. We use it in three main patterns. First, package builds — when an admin calls createAgents, the Lambda publishes to SNS and returns immediately. A separate process Lambda picks up the message and builds the ZIP asynchronously. Second, async investigation queries — when the frontend calls startEdrAsyncQuery, the Lambda publishes to SNS and returns a requestId. A worker Lambda runs the DuckDB query and pushes results back through AppSync subscriptions. Third, package updates — when a new agent version is uploaded to the ingest bucket, an update Lambda fans out SNS messages per organization so all packages get rebuilt. In every case, the API-facing Lambda responds in under a second while the expensive work happens in the background. This is critical for user experience — building an agent package takes 30 to 60 seconds, but the user sees an immediate confirmation.

Follow-up 1: Why SNS over SQS for these patterns?
Answer: Fan-out capability. When a new agent version drops, we need to rebuild packages for potentially hundreds of organizations simultaneously. SNS delivers the message to all subscribers in parallel — the process Lambda gets invoked once per org concurrently. SQS is pull-based and single-consumer by default, so achieving that kind of fan-out would require multiple queues or complex Lambda event source mappings. For the async query pattern, SNS also gives us flexibility — if we ever want to add a logging or metrics subscriber, we just add another subscription without changing the publisher code.

Follow-up 2: How do you handle failures in async processing?
Answer: SNS has a delivery retry policy, and our Lambda functions have dead letter queue configurations. If the worker Lambda fails to process a message after 3 retries, the message goes to an SQS DLQ. We have CloudWatch alarms on DLQ depth — if it goes above zero, we get alerted. For async investigation queries specifically, the worker Lambda catches exceptions and publishes an error result back through AppSync, so the frontend shows 'Query failed' with a reason rather than hanging indefinitely. We also set a timeout on the subscription — if no result arrives within 5 minutes, the frontend times out and suggests the user retry.

============================================================

Q83: How does raw event retrieval work when an analyst wants to see the original event?
Answer: When an analyst clicks on an alert to see the underlying raw event, the frontend calls a GraphQL query like getRawEvent with the event UUID. The resolver Lambda constructs the S3 key using the organization, date, and UUID to locate the gzipped JSON file in the raw events bucket. It reads the object from S3, decompresses the gzip, parses the JSON, and returns the event payload to the frontend. The event contains full process telemetry — the parent process chain, command line arguments, file hashes, network connections, and registry modifications. This is essential for incident investigation because the analyst sees exactly what happened on the endpoint at that moment, not just a summarized alert.

Follow-up 1: How do you efficiently locate a specific event in S3?
Answer: The S3 bucket is organized by prefix: {org}/{date}/{logtype}/{uuid}.json.gz. The UUID is stored in the alert record, so when the analyst views an alert, we already have the UUID. The S3 GetObject call with the exact key is O(1) — no listing or scanning needed. This is why the prefix structure matters — it is not just for organization, it allows us to construct the exact key directly. If we had to list and search through files, retrieval would take seconds instead of milliseconds.

Follow-up 2: Why gzip the events?
Answer: EDR events are verbose JSON — a single process event with its full context, including parent chain, environment variables, and loaded modules, can be 10 to 50 kilobytes. Gzip typically achieves 85 to 90 percent compression on JSON, so a 10KB event becomes about 1.5KB. At our event volume — millions per day across all organizations — the storage and bandwidth savings are substantial. When the Lambda reads an event for display, decompression adds negligible latency, just a few milliseconds. The CPU cost of compression is tiny compared to the network and storage savings.

============================================================

Q84: How do you manage organizations and product enablement?
Answer: We have a console-customer DynamoDB table that tracks which products each organization has enabled — EDR, SIEM, SOAR — and their license status. When an admin logs in, the frontend calls getAllProducts via a GraphQL query, and the Lambda checks this table to determine which modules to show. The setEdrForOrg mutation enables EDR for an org, creating the necessary infrastructure references — DynamoDB entries for the org's agent namespace, S3 prefixes for their packages and policies. There is also an admin-orgs table for the organization hierarchy — parent orgs, child orgs, and org metadata. This supports our channel partner model where a parent organization manages multiple customer orgs. The React frontend conditionally renders navigation and features based on the product and license response.

Follow-up 1: How does the channel partner model work?
Answer: A channel partner is a parent organization that manages multiple customer organizations. In Stytch, the partner has their own organization with admin users. These admins can switch between customer orgs through the console — the frontend calls a context-switching API that issues a new Stytch session scoped to the target org. The resolverContext then reflects the target org, so all data queries return that customer's data. We track the parent-child relationship in the admin-orgs DynamoDB table. This lets managed security service providers operate the platform on behalf of their customers without needing separate deployments.

============================================================

Q85: What is AWS and which services did you use?
Answer: AWS — Amazon Web Services — is a cloud platform that provides computing, storage, networking, and application services on demand. Instead of buying physical servers, you rent capacity from AWS and pay only for what you use. In our EDR platform, we use about 10 AWS services. For compute, we use Lambda — serverless functions that run our backend code. For storage, we use S3 for object storage and DynamoDB for our NoSQL database. For API management, we use API Gateway for the agent REST API and AppSync for the console GraphQL API. For data streaming, we use Kinesis Data Firehose to ingest agent telemetry. For messaging, we use SNS to decouple async operations. For security, we use IAM for permissions, IAM Roles Anywhere for agent credentials, and KMS for encryption keys. For content delivery, we use CloudFront to serve the React frontend. For monitoring, we use CloudWatch for logs, metrics, and alarms.

Follow-up 1: Why AWS over Azure or GCP?
Answer: Primarily team expertise and ecosystem fit. Our team had deep AWS experience, and the services we needed — AppSync for managed GraphQL, Roles Anywhere for agent certificate-based auth, Firehose for streaming ingestion — are mature AWS offerings with strong integration. AppSync subscriptions for real-time push were a deciding factor — Azure and GCP have equivalents, but AppSync's WebSocket management is seamless. That said, most of our patterns — serverless functions, object storage, NoSQL database, pub/sub messaging — have equivalents on all three clouds. The core architecture would transfer.

============================================================

Q86: What is an API and what is the difference between REST and GraphQL?
Answer: An API — Application Programming Interface — is a contract between two systems defining how they communicate. REST and GraphQL are two styles of building APIs. REST organizes endpoints by resource — GET /agents returns all agents, GET /agents/123 returns one agent, POST /agents creates one. Each endpoint returns a fixed data shape. GraphQL is a query language where the client specifies exactly what fields it needs — the client sends a query like '{ agents { name, status } }' and gets exactly those fields, nothing more. In our platform, we use REST for the agent side because agent communication is simple and predictable — enroll, heartbeat, get config. We use GraphQL via AppSync for the console because the dashboard data is complex and nested — agents have zones, zones have policies, policies have rules — and different pages need different fields from the same entities.

Follow-up 1: When would you choose REST over GraphQL?
Answer: REST is simpler for straightforward CRUD operations with predictable data shapes. If every client needs the same data, REST's URL-based caching works great. Our agent API is REST because agents always send the same heartbeat payload and receive the same config response — GraphQL's flexibility would add complexity without benefit. GraphQL shines when you have multiple consumers with different data needs — our console dashboard needs 5 agent fields while the detail page needs 25 fields. With REST, you either over-fetch or build multiple endpoints. GraphQL lets both pages use the same schema with different queries.

============================================================

Q87: What is mutual TLS (mTLS) and how is it different from regular HTTPS?
Answer: Regular HTTPS is one-way TLS — the client verifies the server's identity using the server's certificate, but the server does not verify the client. You experience this every time you visit a website — your browser checks that the site's certificate is valid. Mutual TLS adds the other direction — the server also verifies the client. In our system, when an agent connects to API Gateway, it presents a client certificate that was bundled in its installer package. API Gateway's custom authorizer Lambda extracts the certificate serial number and validates it against our internal certificate authority. If the certificate is invalid or revoked, the connection is rejected. This is stronger than API keys because certificates cannot be easily extracted from network traffic, and they have built-in expiry. If a certificate is compromised, we revoke it at the CA level and the agent can no longer authenticate.

Follow-up 1: Why not use API keys instead of certificates?
Answer: API keys are static strings — if one leaks through logs, memory dumps, or code exposure, anyone can use it until it is rotated. Certificates use asymmetric cryptography — the agent proves it has the private key without ever transmitting it. Even if someone intercepts the TLS handshake, they cannot extract the private key. Certificates also have built-in expiry and revocation through the CA. API keys require manual rotation, which is operationally complex across hundreds of organizations. For machine-to-machine authentication in a security product, certificates are the standard.

============================================================

Q88: What is API Gateway and how did you configure it for agents?
Answer: API Gateway is AWS's managed service for creating, publishing, and managing REST APIs at scale. It handles throttling, authorization, request routing, and SSL termination. For our agent stack, we configured a REST API type with mutual TLS enabled — API Gateway terminates the TLS connection and passes the client certificate to our Lambda authorizer. Routes include POST /enroll, POST /doheartbeat, GET /get_config, GET /getPolicy, and POST /getHunt. Each route maps to our agent Lambda. The authorizer Lambda runs first on every request, validates the certificate, and returns an IAM Allow policy with the org context. We also configured throttling limits per route — heartbeats have a higher allowed rate than enrollment calls because enrollment happens once per agent while heartbeats happen every 60 seconds.

Follow-up 1: What is throttling and why is it important?
Answer: Throttling limits the number of API requests per second to protect backend resources. Without it, a misconfigured agent or a DDoS attack could overwhelm our Lambda with millions of requests. API Gateway lets us set rate limits per route — for example, 10,000 heartbeats per second across all agents but only 100 enrollments per second. Requests exceeding the limit get a 429 Too Many Requests response. The agent retries with exponential backoff. This protects both our Lambda concurrency pool and DynamoDB write capacity from spikes.

Follow-up 2: Why REST API type instead of HTTP API?
Answer: REST APIs support Lambda authorizers that return IAM policies with custom context — we need to pass the certificate serial number and org mapping to the backend Lambda. HTTP APIs support simpler JWT authorizers but not the certificate-based custom auth we require. REST APIs also support request validation and usage plans. We primarily chose it for the authorizer flexibility — the ability to extract certificate data and return structured context was essential for our mutual TLS setup.

============================================================

Q89: What is AWS AppSync and why did you choose it over REST?
Answer: AppSync is AWS's managed GraphQL service. It handles the GraphQL server, connection management, and real-time subscriptions — all fully managed. We chose it for three reasons. First, our console domain is deeply nested — an agent has a zone, which has policies, which have rules — and GraphQL's type system models this naturally. Second, AppSync subscriptions give us real-time push for async query results without us managing WebSocket servers or infrastructure. When a DuckDB query finishes, the result is pushed to the frontend instantly through the subscription. Third, we use a single Lambda resolver pattern — one Lambda handles all GraphQL operations and routes internally based on event.info.fieldName. This keeps deployment simple. The trade-off is some vendor lock-in and slightly higher latency than a direct Lambda URL, but the subscription support alone justified the choice.

Follow-up 1: What are AppSync subscriptions?
Answer: Subscriptions are a GraphQL feature for real-time updates. The frontend sends a subscription query like onEdrAsyncQueryResponse(requestId) and AppSync opens a WebSocket connection. When the backend calls a matching mutation — in our case, edrAsyncQueryResponse — AppSync pushes the result to all subscribers matching that requestId. In practice, when our DuckDB worker Lambda finishes processing a query, it calls the mutation with IAM auth, and AppSync pushes the progress and results to the React frontend in real time. The analyst sees a progress bar filling up and then results appearing — no polling, no manual refreshing needed.

Follow-up 2: What is a GraphQL resolver?
Answer: A resolver is the function that fetches data for a specific GraphQL field. In AppSync, each field in your schema — getAgentsList, addPolicy, downloadAgent — maps to a resolver. We use a single Lambda as the data source for all our resolvers. The Lambda receives event.info.fieldName, which tells it which operation is being called, and it routes internally to the correct handler function. This is called the single-resolver or router pattern. The alternative would be using direct DynamoDB resolvers with VTL templates, but our Lambda gives us more flexibility for complex business logic like policy transformations and cross-service calls.

============================================================

Q90: What is AWS Lambda and how did you use multiple Lambdas?
Answer: Lambda is serverless compute — you upload your code, AWS runs it on demand, and you pay only per invocation. No servers to manage. We have six or more Lambdas, each with a single responsibility. The agent Lambda handles all agent REST calls — enrollment, heartbeats, config, and policy. The API user Lambda resolves all AppSync GraphQL operations for the console. The authentication Lambda validates Stytch JWTs and returns the resolver context. The cert authorizer validates agent mTLS certificates. The process Lambda builds org-specific agent packages, triggered by SNS. And the subscriptions Lambda runs DuckDB async queries, also triggered by SNS. Each Lambda has its own IAM role with least-privilege permissions — the agent Lambda can read policies but cannot touch the AppSync API, and the query worker can read the data lake but cannot modify DynamoDB.

Follow-up 1: What is a cold start and how did you handle it?
Answer: A cold start is the delay when Lambda creates a new execution environment — downloading your code, initializing the runtime, and running your initialization code. For Python Lambdas it is typically 200 to 500 milliseconds. We mitigate it with provisioned concurrency on the AppSync resolver Lambda because that is our most latency-sensitive function — analysts expect instant responses when clicking around the console. For the agent Lambda, cold starts are acceptable because agents automatically retry on failure. We also moved heavy imports like boto3 clients and database connections to module level so they persist across warm invocations and only run once.

Follow-up 2: What is the difference between Lambda and EC2?
Answer: EC2 is a virtual server — you manage the operating system, scaling, patching, and you pay whether it is busy or idle. Lambda is event-driven — you just provide code, AWS manages everything else, and you pay only when your code runs. We chose Lambda because our workloads are bursty: thousands of heartbeats per minute during business hours, near zero at night. With EC2, we would need auto-scaling groups and would still over-provision during off hours. Lambda also simplifies deployment — upload a ZIP and you are done, no server configuration. The trade-offs are a 15-minute max execution time and 10GB memory limit, which are fine for our request-response patterns.

============================================================

Q91: What is DynamoDB and why single-table design?
Answer: DynamoDB is a fully managed NoSQL key-value and document database from AWS. It scales horizontally and provides single-digit millisecond reads and writes at any scale. We use a single-table design — one table holds agents, zones, policies, packages, and policy mappings. The partition key encodes the entity type, like AGENT##{org}, ZONE##{org}, or POLICYMAPPING##{org}, and the sort key is the entity ID. This lets us fetch all related items in a single query — for example, all agents for an organization is just a query on partition key AGENT##acme with no sort key filter. Single-table avoids the need for cross-table joins, which DynamoDB does not support, and reduces the number of tables we need to manage.

Follow-up 1: What is a partition key vs sort key?
Answer: The partition key determines which physical partition stores the data — DynamoDB hashes it to distribute data across servers. The sort key orders items within a partition. Together they form the primary key. In our table, PK equals AGENT##acme and SK equals endpoint-123 uniquely identifies one agent. Querying on PK equals AGENT##acme returns all agents for that organization, sorted by endpoint ID. This is why we prefix partition keys with entity type — it groups related data on the same partition for efficient queries.

Follow-up 2: What is eventual consistency vs strong consistency in DynamoDB?
Answer: DynamoDB replicates data across three availability zones. Eventually consistent reads might return slightly stale data — milliseconds behind the latest write. Strongly consistent reads always return the latest write but cost twice the read capacity. We use eventual consistency for agent list queries because showing a heartbeat that is 200 milliseconds old is perfectly fine. But we use strong consistency for policy updates — when an agent calls getPolicy, it must get the latest version, not a stale one. The getPolicy handler does a ConsistentRead equals true on the policy mapping item.

Follow-up 3: How did you handle cross-region DynamoDB for Prod3?
Answer: Prod3 serves Mumbai customers in ap-south-1, but some tables live in us-east-1 for centralized management. We use DynamoDB Global Tables for the agent metadata table — writes in either region replicate automatically with last-writer-wins conflict resolution. For tables that do not need cross-region writes, like admin configuration, the Lambda in ap-south-1 simply points to the us-east-1 table endpoint. This is configured through environment variables — the DYNAMO_REGION can differ from the Lambda's deployment region.

============================================================

Q92: What is Amazon S3 and how did you use 5 different buckets?
Answer: S3 — Simple Storage Service — is object storage. It is infinitely scalable, extremely durable at eleven nines, and cost-effective. We use five buckets with distinct purposes. The Admin Agent Bucket stores the raw binaries — MSI, deb, rpm — for each OS and version. The Customer Agent Bucket stores org-specific ZIPs that include the binary plus config plus certificates — this is what users download. The Policy Bucket stores policy JSON files referenced by DynamoDB policy mappings. The Raw Events Bucket receives gzipped JSON from Kinesis Firehose — all the agent telemetry. And the Data Lake Bucket stores Parquet files transformed from raw events, used for analytics queries. Each bucket has separate IAM policies — the agent Lambda can read policies but cannot touch raw events, and the query worker can read the data lake but cannot modify policies.

Follow-up 1: What is a presigned URL and why did you use it?
Answer: A presigned URL is a time-limited URL that grants temporary access to a private S3 object. Instead of making the customer agent bucket public, which would be a security risk, the downloadAgent Lambda generates a presigned URL valid for 15 minutes. The frontend redirects the browser to this URL, and S3 serves the ZIP file directly to the user. The URL includes a cryptographic signature — if someone modifies the path or the URL expires, S3 rejects the request. This is how we securely serve agent packages that can be 50 to 100 megabytes without exposing the bucket to the public internet.

Follow-up 2: What is S3 lifecycle policy?
Answer: Lifecycle rules automatically transition or delete objects based on age. Our raw events bucket has a lifecycle: after 30 days, objects move from S3 Standard to S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval, which is about 80 percent cheaper. After one year, they move to Glacier Deep Archive. After three years, they are deleted per our compliance policy. The data lake bucket keeps Parquet files in Standard for 90 days, then moves them to Infrequent Access. This tiered approach saves significant cost — raw event storage is our largest AWS bill item, so lifecycle policies directly reduce operating expenses.

============================================================

Q93: What is Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose and why did you use it?
Answer: Firehose is a fully managed service for loading streaming data into destinations like S3. Our agents send raw EDR events — process creation, file writes, network connections — to Firehose, and it automatically batches them, compresses them with gzip, and delivers them to our raw events S3 bucket. We chose Firehose over having agents do direct S3 PutObject calls for three reasons. First, Firehose handles buffering — agents send events individually but Firehose batches them into efficient S3 writes, reducing the number of objects. Second, it handles retries automatically if S3 is temporarily unavailable. Third, it handles the compression for us. The agent gets the Firehose stream name from get_config and uses its IAM Roles Anywhere credentials to write to it. This keeps the agent lightweight — it just fires events to the stream and moves on.

Follow-up 1: What is the difference between Kinesis Data Streams and Firehose?
Answer: Kinesis Data Streams is a raw streaming buffer — you manage shards, consumers, and checkpointing, and you can have multiple consumers reading the same data. Firehose is a delivery pipe — it has one destination and manages everything automatically. We chose Firehose because we have a single destination, which is S3, and we do not need multiple consumers at the ingestion layer. Downstream processing happens on the S3 files, not on the stream. Firehose is also fully serverless — no shard management, no capacity planning — which aligned with our goal of minimizing operational overhead.

============================================================

Q94: What is Amazon SNS and how did you use it for event-driven architecture?
Answer: SNS — Simple Notification Service — is a pub/sub messaging service. You publish a message to a topic, and all subscribers receive it. We use two SNS topics. The Package Build Topic — when the createAgents mutation runs, the Lambda publishes a message. The process Lambda is subscribed and builds the org-specific ZIP. This decouples the API response from the slow build process so the user is not waiting. The Async Query Topic — when the frontend starts an investigation query, the API Lambda publishes the query payload to this topic. The DuckDB worker Lambda is subscribed and processes the query in the background. This lets us return a requestId immediately while the heavy computation runs asynchronously.

Follow-up 1: Why SNS instead of directly invoking the Lambda?
Answer: Three reasons. First, decoupling — the publisher does not know or care who processes the message. If we want to add a logging subscriber later, we just add a subscription without changing the publisher. Second, retry handling — if the worker Lambda fails, SNS retries with exponential backoff automatically. Direct invocation would require us to implement our own retry logic. Third, fan-out — when we need to rebuild packages for hundreds of organizations at once, SNS delivers the message to all subscribers in parallel.

Follow-up 2: What is the difference between SNS and SQS?
Answer: SNS is push-based — messages are delivered immediately to all subscribers. SQS is pull-based — consumers poll for messages from a queue. SNS is for broadcasting to multiple consumers; SQS is for buffering work for a single consumer. We use SNS because we want immediate processing — when a package build is requested, the worker should start right away. If we needed rate limiting or wanted to process messages in controlled batches, we would put SQS between SNS and Lambda, creating an SNS to SQS to Lambda chain. That pattern would help if we ever hit Lambda concurrency limits during peak periods.

============================================================

Q95: What is Stytch and how did you use it for B2B authentication?
Answer: Stytch is a B2B authentication platform — similar to Auth0 but specifically designed for multi-tenant SaaS products. It handles user signup, login, session management, and organization-level access control. In our system, the React frontend authenticates with Stytch and receives a session JWT. This JWT goes to AppSync with every request. AppSync invokes our auth Lambda, which calls Stytch's API to validate the session and extract claims — the user's email, organization_id, organization_slug, organization_region, and organization_type. These become the resolverContext that every GraphQL resolver receives. So every query is automatically scoped to the correct organization without the frontend explicitly passing org information.

Follow-up 1: Why Stytch over building your own auth?
Answer: Authentication is security-critical and deceptively complex — password hashing, session invalidation, rate limiting, brute-force protection, SSO, and MFA. Building all of that yourself means maintaining it forever. Stytch gives us B2B-specific features out of the box: organization management where each customer is a Stytch organization, member roles, SSO with customer identity providers, and session tokens with org context baked in. The cost is a dependency on an external service, but the security and development time savings easily justified it.

Follow-up 2: What is a resolverContext?
Answer: In AppSync, when a Lambda authorizer approves a request, it can attach key-value pairs called resolverContext. These are passed to every resolver invocation in event.identity.resolverContext. We attach the user's email for audit logging, organization_id for data scoping, organization_slug for constructing DynamoDB keys, organization_region for routing to the right data endpoints, and organization_type to enable or disable features based on the customer's plan. This is how tenant isolation works — the resolver always knows which organization is making the request, and this information comes from the server-side auth Lambda, not from the client. The frontend never sends org_id in its requests.

============================================================

Q96: What is Multi-Tenancy and how did you implement it?
Answer: Multi-tenancy means one system serves multiple customers — called tenants — using shared infrastructure. Each tenant's data must be completely isolated. Org A should never see Org B's agents or alerts. We implement isolation at three layers. First, authentication — Stytch JWTs carry the org_id, and the auth Lambda extracts it into the resolverContext. Second, the data layer — DynamoDB partition keys are org-scoped, like AGENT##acme and AGENT##globex. A query on partition key AGENT##acme physically cannot return Org B's data because they are on different partitions. Third, the application layer — every Lambda handler reads the organization from the resolverContext, never from user input. There is no admin query that could accidentally return all organizations' data. This three-layer approach means even if one layer has a bug, the others still protect tenant isolation.

Follow-up 1: What are different multi-tenancy models?
Answer: There are three main models. Silo means each tenant gets their own separate database and infrastructure — maximum isolation but very expensive to operate. Bridge means shared infrastructure but separate schemas or tables per tenant. Pool means shared everything, with isolation enforced through row-level filtering. We use the Pool model — one DynamoDB table, one set of Lambdas, and isolation through partition key prefixing. The Silo model would mean maintaining hundreds of separate DynamoDB tables, one per organization, which is unmanageable. Pool works for us because DynamoDB's partition key design naturally isolates data access patterns at the storage level.

============================================================

Q97: What is DuckDB and how do you use it for analytics and investigation queries?
Answer: DuckDB is an embedded analytical database engine — it runs directly inside a Lambda function with no external server or cluster needed. We use it for both dashboard analytics and ad-hoc investigation queries. When an analyst runs an investigation query like 'show me all process creation events from this host in the last 7 days,' DuckDB reads the relevant Parquet files directly from S3 using its httpfs extension, runs the SQL query in-process, and returns results. It starts in milliseconds because there is no cluster to warm up, it processes gigabytes of Parquet data efficiently using columnar optimizations, and it costs nothing beyond the Lambda execution time. The key to making it fast is the Dragonfly file index — instead of scanning the entire data lake, we first look up which Parquet files contain the data we need, and DuckDB reads only those specific files.

Follow-up 1: How does DuckDB read files from S3 inside Lambda?
