Answer: DuckDB has an httpfs extension that lets it read S3 objects as if they were local files. We configure it with the Lambda's IAM role credentials, which it inherits from the execution role. The SQL looks something like SELECT * FROM read_parquet('s3://bucket/path/*.parquet') WHERE host equals 'xyz'. The important thing is that DuckDB does not download entire files — it reads only the necessary column chunks and row groups from each Parquet file. Combined with the Dragonfly file index that tells us exactly which files to query, DuckDB only touches the relevant data, which is what makes queries return in seconds.

Follow-up 2: What is Apache Iceberg?
Answer: Iceberg is an open table format for large analytic datasets stored on S3. Think of it as adding database-like features on top of plain Parquet files — schema evolution, partition management, and the ability to skip irrelevant files during queries. Without a table format, querying Parquet on S3 means scanning everything. With Iceberg, the query engine reads metadata to know which files contain relevant data, so a query for 'alerts in the last 24 hours' only reads yesterday's partition, not the entire history. This metadata layer is a big part of why our queries return quickly despite having terabytes of data in the lake.

Follow-up 3: What is Parquet and why is it important?
Answer: Parquet is a columnar file format. Traditional formats like CSV and JSON store data row by row. Parquet stores data column by column. If your query only needs 3 columns out of 50, Parquet reads only those 3 columns — drastically reducing how much data is read from S3. It also supports per-column compression and stores min/max statistics for each column chunk, which lets query engines skip chunks that do not match filter conditions. Our data pipeline converts raw JSON events into Parquet files partitioned by org_id and date. This columnar format is what makes DuckDB queries fast — it reads minimal data from S3.

============================================================

Q98: How does the async investigation query flow work end-to-end with DuckDB?
Answer: Let me walk through the complete flow. An analyst on the console wants to investigate suspicious activity on a specific host. They enter their query parameters and click Investigate. The React frontend calls the startEdrAsyncQuery GraphQL mutation through AppSync. The resolver Lambda publishes the query payload to our Async Query SNS topic and immediately returns a requestId — the analyst is not blocked. The frontend then subscribes to onEdrAsyncQueryResponse using that requestId via an AppSync WebSocket subscription. On the backend, our worker Lambda gets triggered by the SNS message. First, it queries the Dragonfly file index to find which Parquet files in S3 contain the relevant data — looking up keys like file_index:{org}:{date}:{logtype}. Then it initializes DuckDB with the httpfs extension, reads those specific Parquet files from S3, and runs the SQL query. As it processes, it publishes progress updates back to AppSync through an IAM-signed GraphQL mutation. The frontend receives these updates through its subscription and shows a progress bar. When the query completes, the worker publishes the final results, and the analyst sees them instantly. The whole process typically takes 5 to 30 seconds depending on how much data is being scanned.

Follow-up 1: What happens if the DuckDB query takes too long?
Answer: Lambda has a 15-minute maximum execution time. If a query approaches this limit — which happens with very large date ranges across many log types — the worker Lambda has a timeout handler that returns partial results along with a message like 'query too large, try narrowing the date range.' On the frontend, the subscription timeout is set to 5 minutes. If nothing comes back in 5 minutes, the frontend shows a timeout message and suggests the analyst retry with more specific filters. For very large investigations, analysts typically narrow their search by using specific date ranges, specific event types, or specific hosts rather than broad queries.

============================================================

Q99: What is Dragonfly and why did you use it as a file index?
Answer: Dragonfly is a Redis-compatible in-memory database that is faster and more memory-efficient than Redis itself. We use it as a file index — it maps the combination of organization, date, and log type to the S3 Parquet file paths that contain that data. Keys look like file_index:{org}:{pdate}:{logtype} and values are lists of S3 paths. When our DuckDB worker Lambda needs to query 'all process events for Org X on March 5th,' it asks Dragonfly for the file list in microseconds, then passes those specific S3 paths to DuckDB. Without this index, DuckDB would need to list the S3 bucket — which is very slow when you have millions of files — or scan everything, which would be expensive and wasteful.

Follow-up 1: Why Dragonfly instead of Redis?
Answer: Dragonfly is API-compatible with Redis but uses a multi-threaded architecture and more efficient memory management. For the same workload, Dragonfly uses about 30 to 40 percent less memory and handles more operations per second. Since our file index is read-heavy — thousands of lookups per minute from the async query workers — the performance improvement matters. The migration was trivial because it uses the same Redis client libraries and the same commands. We just pointed our code to the Dragonfly endpoint instead of Redis.

Follow-up 2: What is Redis and what are its typical use cases?
Answer: Redis is an in-memory key-value store where data lives in RAM, making reads microsecond-fast. Common use cases include caching to avoid repeated database queries, session storage, rate limiting, pub/sub messaging, and leaderboards. In our system, we also use Valkey — another Redis-compatible store — in the auth Lambda to cache the list of organizations with special configurations. The auth Lambda checks Valkey before calling Stytch — if the org config is cached, we skip an API call and save 50 to 100 milliseconds per request.

============================================================

Q100: What is Valkey and how is it different from Redis?
Answer: Valkey is a Redis fork created after Redis changed its open-source license. It is fully API-compatible but remains open-source under the BSD license. AWS ElastiCache now offers Valkey as a drop-in Redis replacement. In our auth Lambda, Valkey caches organization configurations. When a request comes in, the Lambda checks Valkey for the org's feature flags, region routing, and plan type before calling Stytch. This avoids an external API call on every request. The cache TTL is 5 minutes, so configuration changes propagate within that window.

============================================================

Q101: What is IAM Roles Anywhere and why did you use it for agents?
Answer: IAM Roles Anywhere lets workloads outside of AWS — like our agents running on customer endpoints — get temporary AWS credentials using X.509 certificates. Normally, only AWS services like Lambda and EC2 can assume IAM roles. With Roles Anywhere, the agent presents its client certificate to a trust anchor, proves its identity, and receives temporary credentials — access key, secret key, and session token — scoped to a specific IAM role. The agent uses these credentials to write to Kinesis Firehose. The credentials expire in one hour and auto-renew. This is much more secure than embedding long-lived AWS access keys in the agent binary, which could be extracted by anyone who has the installer.

Follow-up 1: What is the difference between IAM Roles Anywhere and access keys?
Answer: Access keys are permanent credentials — if they are leaked, an attacker has indefinite access until you manually rotate them. IAM Roles Anywhere issues temporary credentials with a one-hour TTL tied to a specific certificate. If the certificate is revoked or the agent is decommissioned, it simply cannot get new credentials. It follows the same principle as EC2 instance profiles — temporary, auto-rotating, no secrets to manage — but extended to devices that run outside of AWS.

============================================================

Q102: What is MITRE ATT&CK and how did you use it?
Answer: MITRE ATT&CK is a knowledge base of adversary tactics and techniques based on real-world observations. It categorizes attacks into Tactics — the 'why,' like Initial Access, Persistence, or Lateral Movement — and Techniques — the 'how,' like T1059 Command and Scripting Interpreter. In our platform, every detection rule is mapped to specific MITRE techniques. When an alert fires, the dashboard shows which tactic and technique was detected. Our analytics queries like getTacticsOverview and getTopMitreAttCkTTPs let analysts see which attack patterns are most common across their endpoints. The Rule Builder also lets users tag rules with MITRE IDs during creation, so there is a complete chain from rule authoring to alert display.

============================================================

Q103: What is AWS CDK and how was it used for infrastructure?
Answer: CDK — Cloud Development Kit — lets you define AWS infrastructure using programming languages like Python or TypeScript instead of writing JSON or YAML templates. Our CDK app defines both stacks — the agent stack with API Gateway, Lambda, and the authorizer, and the API stack with AppSync, Lambda, and auth — in Python. Each environment like dev, prod1, and prod3 is a separate stack instantiation with different parameters for region, table names, and bucket names. CDK synthesizes to CloudFormation templates and deploys them. The benefit over raw CloudFormation is code reuse — our Lambda construct is a Python class that configures the IAM role, VPC settings, environment variables, and layers. We instantiate it once per function instead of copying YAML blocks.

Follow-up 1: What is CloudFormation?
Answer: CloudFormation is AWS's infrastructure-as-code service. You write a template describing resources — Lambda functions, DynamoDB tables, S3 buckets — and AWS creates and updates them for you. CDK is a higher-level abstraction that generates CloudFormation templates. When you run cdk deploy, it synthesizes a CloudFormation template and submits it. CloudFormation handles dependency ordering — creating the DynamoDB table before the Lambda that reads it — rollback on failure, and drift detection. Our prod3 stack's CloudFormation template is about 3000 lines of YAML, which would be extremely error-prone to write by hand.

============================================================

Q104: What is a Custom Authorizer in API Gateway?
Answer: A custom authorizer, also called a Lambda authorizer, is a Lambda function that API Gateway invokes before routing any request to the backend. It receives the request — headers, body, certificates — and returns an IAM policy with Allow or Deny plus optional context data. Our cert authorizer receives the client certificate from the mTLS handshake, extracts the serial number, looks up the org mapping in DynamoDB, and returns Allow with a context containing the org_slug. API Gateway caches the authorization result for the certificate's TTL, so repeated requests from the same agent do not re-invoke the authorizer every time. This is how we authenticate agents without tokens or API keys — identity comes from the certificate itself.

============================================================

Q105: What is a Data Lake and how is yours structured?
Answer: A data lake is centralized storage for raw and processed data at any scale, typically built on S3. Ours has two layers. The raw layer contains gzipped JSON files delivered by Kinesis Firehose, organized by date prefix — year, month, day, hour. The processed layer contains Parquet files in Iceberg table format, partitioned by org_id and date. The raw-to-processed transformation is handled by an external pipeline outside our codebase. The processed layer is what DuckDB queries when analysts run investigations. The Dragonfly file index maps logical queries — org plus date plus event type — to physical Parquet file paths in S3, which is what enables fast query planning.

Follow-up 1: What is the difference between a data lake and a data warehouse?
Answer: A data warehouse like Redshift or BigQuery stores structured, pre-processed data in a proprietary format — you load data into it. A data lake stores raw data in open formats like Parquet and JSON on cheap storage like S3 — you bring compute to it. Our data lake uses S3, Parquet, and Iceberg — all open formats — and we bring DuckDB as our compute engine. This avoids vendor lock-in and lets us choose the right tool for the job. The trade-off is that we manage the table format ourselves instead of relying on a fully managed warehouse, but the flexibility and cost savings are worth it.

============================================================

Q106: What is event-driven architecture and where did you use it?
Answer: Event-driven architecture means components communicate through events — messages — instead of direct calls. In our system, we use this pattern in three places. First, package creation: the API Lambda publishes to SNS, and a process Lambda builds the ZIP asynchronously. The API returns immediately. Second, async investigation queries: the API Lambda publishes to SNS, the DuckDB worker processes the query, and pushes results back through AppSync subscriptions. Third, new package uploads: when a new binary is uploaded to S3, an S3 event notification triggers an update Lambda. The benefit is loose coupling — the API does not know about the worker, and natural scalability — 100 concurrent queries means 100 Lambda invocations running in parallel, no bottleneck. The challenge is observability — tracing a request across SNS to Lambda to AppSync requires correlation IDs so you can follow the flow in logs.

Follow-up 1: What is a correlation ID?
Answer: A correlation ID is a unique identifier, usually a UUID, generated at the start of a request and passed through every service in the chain. When the frontend starts an async query, it generates a requestId. The API Lambda passes it to the SNS message, the worker Lambda logs it, and the AppSync subscription uses it to route results back to the right client. If something fails, we search CloudWatch logs for that requestId to trace the entire flow across all our Lambdas. Without correlation IDs, debugging distributed systems would mean trying to match events by timestamp, which is unreliable and painful.

============================================================

Q107: What are zones and policies in your EDR system?
Answer: Zones are logical groupings of endpoints — like 'Engineering Laptops', 'Production Servers', or 'Finance Workstations'. Each zone has a default policy for Windows and a default policy for Linux. A policy defines what the agent monitors on the endpoint: which event types to collect — process, file, network, registry — which detection rules to apply, and what response actions are allowed. When an agent calls getPolicy, the Lambda looks up the agent's zone in DynamoDB, finds the policy mapping for that zone and platform, reads the policy JSON from S3, and returns it. This lets admins apply different security levels to different groups: servers get aggressive monitoring, developer laptops get lighter policies to reduce performance impact.

Follow-up 1: How does moving an agent between zones work?
Answer: An admin calls the addAgentsToZone mutation with the agent IDs and the target zone. The Lambda updates each agent's DynamoDB record with the new zoneid. On the next heartbeat, the agent's policyHash is compared against the new zone's policy. Since it will not match, the heartbeat response signals the agent to call getPolicy. The agent fetches the new zone's policy and applies it. The maximum propagation delay is one heartbeat interval — 60 seconds. No agent restart is needed because policies are hot-reloaded by the agent.

============================================================

Q108: What is a presigned URL and how does agent download work end-to-end?
Answer: When a user clicks 'Download Agent' in the console, the frontend calls the downloadAgent GraphQL mutation with the org_slug and filepath. The Lambda uses the AWS SDK to generate a presigned URL — a regular HTTPS URL with a query string containing a cryptographic signature, expiry time, and the caller's credentials. This URL grants temporary read access to that specific S3 object for 15 minutes. The frontend receives the URL and redirects the browser to it. S3 validates the signature and serves the ZIP file directly to the user. No proxy Lambda needed, no public bucket needed. If someone shares the URL after 15 minutes, it simply stops working. The file goes directly from S3 to the user's browser.

============================================================

Q109: What are lookups and macros in the EDR platform?
Answer: Lookups are reference lists used in detection rules — for example, a list of known-malicious IP addresses, trusted process hashes, or admin user accounts. When a detection rule evaluates an event, it can check whether a value is in one of these lists. Macros are reusable query fragments — instead of repeating the same condition in 20 rules, like 'process.name NOT IN (svchost.exe, csrss.exe, lsass.exe),' you define a macro called 'known_system_processes' and reference it. Both are stored in DynamoDB per organization and included in the policy payload. This makes rule authoring faster and maintenance easier — update one lookup or macro and all rules using it get the change on the next policy fetch.

Follow-up 1: How do lookups differ from the Rule Builder's Lookup Lists?
Answer: They serve similar purposes but operate in different contexts. OneAgent lookups are embedded in the agent policy — the agent checks events locally against these lists on the endpoint itself. Rule Builder Lookup Lists are server-side — they are referenced in Flink SQL queries that process events in the streaming pipeline. So OneAgent lookups enable edge detection, happening right on the endpoint, while Rule Builder lists enable centralized detection in the cloud pipeline. An organization might use both: agent-side bloom filters for fast local matching of known indicators, and server-side Flink lookups for complex multi-field correlation that the agent cannot do.

============================================================

Q110: What is the difference between synchronous and asynchronous operations in your system?
Answer: Synchronous operations follow a request-response pattern — the frontend calls a GraphQL query, the Lambda processes it, and returns the result immediately. Examples include getAgentsList, viewAgent, and addPolicy. Response times are typically 100 to 500 milliseconds. Asynchronous operations are different. The frontend calls a mutation like startEdrAsyncQuery, the Lambda publishes to SNS and returns a requestId immediately in about 100 milliseconds. A worker Lambda processes the query in the background — this can take 5 to 60 seconds — and pushes the result back through an AppSync subscription. We use async for anything that takes more than 2 seconds. The user gets immediate feedback like 'Query started' and then sees real-time progress updates instead of staring at a spinner.

Follow-up 1: When should you use async over sync?
Answer: Use sync when the operation is fast and predictable — under 2 seconds. Fetching agent lists, viewing policies, updating a zone — these are all quick DynamoDB operations. Use async when the operation is slow or unpredictable: DuckDB queries over large Parquet datasets, package builds that involve downloading and zipping files from S3, or any fan-out operation. The rule of thumb is: if it might hit the Lambda 15-minute timeout or make the user wait more than 2 seconds, make it async with progress feedback. Async also gives you natural retry capability through SNS, which synchronous invocations do not have.

============================================================

Q111: What is GraphQL and how is it different from REST?
Answer: GraphQL is a query language for APIs where the client specifies exactly what data it needs. In REST, each endpoint returns a fixed shape — GET /agents returns all fields for all agents. In GraphQL, the client sends a query like '{ agents { name, status, zone { name } } }' and gets exactly those fields, nothing more and nothing less. In our platform, the agents list page queries only 5 fields per agent, but the detail page queries 25 fields — they use the same schema, just different queries. This avoids over-fetching, which is especially important for our dashboard with many widgets making different requests. The trade-off is that caching is harder — REST uses URL-based HTTP caching, while GraphQL needs client-side caching solutions like Apollo Client's normalized cache.

Follow-up 1: What are the downsides of GraphQL?
Answer: Caching is harder because every request is a POST to the same URL, so HTTP-level caching does not work — you need client-side solutions like Apollo's normalized cache. Security is a concern — without query depth limits, a malicious client could send deeply nested queries that overwhelm the server. Monitoring is trickier because all requests hit the same endpoint, so you cannot distinguish operations from HTTP logs alone. And there is a learning curve with the type system, resolvers, and schema design. We mitigate the security concern with our single-resolver pattern which avoids nested resolver chains, and use Apollo Client for client-side caching.

============================================================

Q112: What is IAM (Identity and Access Management) and how did you use least-privilege?
Answer: IAM is AWS's permission system — it controls who can do what on which resources. Every Lambda function has an IAM execution role that defines its permissions. We follow the principle of least privilege: the agent Lambda can read and write the agent DynamoDB table and read the policy S3 bucket, but it cannot access the AppSync API or the raw events bucket. The cert authorizer can only read DynamoDB, no writes. The async query worker can read the S3 data lake and call AppSync mutations to publish results, but cannot modify DynamoDB. If one Lambda is compromised, the blast radius is limited to only its specific permissions. We define these roles in CDK with explicit resource ARNs — no wildcards allowed.

Follow-up 1: What happens if a Lambda has overly permissive IAM?
Answer: If a Lambda has permissions like s3:* or dynamodb:* and that Lambda is compromised — for example through a dependency vulnerability — the attacker can read and write all S3 buckets and all DynamoDB tables in the account, including other customers' data. With least-privilege, a compromised agent Lambda can only access agent-specific tables and buckets. We enforce this in CDK — each Lambda construct receives explicit resource ARNs, and we have a review process that flags wildcard permissions in pull requests.

============================================================

Q113: What is a Dead Letter Queue (DLQ) and where did you use it?
Answer: A DLQ is a queue where messages that fail processing are sent instead of being lost. Our SNS topics have DLQ configuration — if the worker Lambda fails to process a message after 3 retries, the message goes to an SQS DLQ. We monitor DLQ depth in CloudWatch and have an alarm that fires if it goes above zero. This lets us investigate failures without losing data — we can read the failed message, understand what went wrong, fix the bug, and replay the message. For async investigation queries, a DLQ failure means the analyst's query silently failed, so we added a CloudWatch alarm that notifies us proactively so we can reach out before the analyst even reports the issue.

Follow-up 1: How do you replay messages from the DLQ?
Answer: We read the failed message from the SQS DLQ, inspect its contents, and check the Lambda's CloudWatch logs to understand the failure reason — usually a code bug or a transient AWS error. After fixing the issue, we can either manually re-publish the message to the original SNS topic, or use the SQS DLQ redrive feature to move messages back to the source. For async queries, we also notify the analyst that their query failed and provide a retry button on the frontend.

============================================================

Q114: What is CloudWatch and how did you use it for observability?
Answer: CloudWatch is AWS's monitoring service — it collects logs, metrics, and alarms. Every Lambda automatically sends logs to CloudWatch Log Groups. We add structured JSON logging with correlation IDs so we can trace a single request across multiple Lambda invocations. We also track custom metrics like agent enrollment rate, heartbeat processing latency, async query duration, and DuckDB query data size. CloudWatch Alarms trigger when metrics cross thresholds — for example, heartbeat Lambda error rate above 1 percent or DLQ depth above zero. Dashboards show real-time operational health across all our services.

Follow-up 1: How do you use CloudWatch Insights for debugging?
Answer: CloudWatch Insights lets you write SQL-like queries over your structured logs. For example, you can search for a specific correlation ID across all Lambda log groups to trace a single request end-to-end. Or you can aggregate errors by type to find the most common failures. Or you can calculate average duration by operation to identify slow handlers. It is our first tool when investigating production issues — much faster than scrolling through raw log streams. We use it daily to monitor the health of the async query pipeline and the agent heartbeat flow.

============================================================

Q115: What is gzip compression and where did you use it?
Answer: Gzip is a lossless compression algorithm that typically reduces text data size by 70 to 90 percent. We use it in several places. First, Kinesis Firehose compresses raw JSON events with gzip before writing to S3 — a 10KB JSON event becomes about 1.5KB, saving significant storage cost. Second, when the getRawEvent GraphQL query fetches an event from S3, the Lambda decompresses the gzip on the fly and returns the JSON to the frontend. Third, API Gateway supports gzip response compression — large agent lists are compressed before sending to the React frontend, reducing bandwidth. The CPU cost of compression is negligible compared to the network and storage savings, especially at our event volume of millions of events per day.

Follow-up 1: What are the trade-offs of compression?
Answer: The main trade-off is CPU cost versus I/O savings. Gzip adds about 5 milliseconds of compression time per event on the agent side but saves 70 to 90 percent in bandwidth. For Lambda decompression, it is negligible — just a few milliseconds for a single event. A bigger consideration is query performance — DuckDB has to decompress gzipped files before reading them. Parquet handles this better because it supports per-column compression with Snappy, which is optimized for fast decompression rather than high compression ratio. So we use gzip for raw JSON ingestion where space savings matter most, and Snappy compression inside Parquet files where read speed matters most.

============================================================

Q116: What is serverless architecture and why did you choose it?
Answer: Serverless means the cloud provider manages all the servers, scaling, and patching — you just write code. In our platform, Lambda is serverless compute, DynamoDB is serverless database, Firehose is serverless streaming, and AppSync is serverless GraphQL. We chose serverless because our workloads are bursty: thousands of heartbeats during business hours, near zero at night. With EC2, we would pay for idle capacity during off hours. With Lambda, we pay only per request. The trade-offs are cold starts, which we mitigate with provisioned concurrency, a 15-minute max execution time, which is fine for our request-response patterns, and vendor lock-in, which we accepted because the team velocity gain was worth it.

Follow-up 1: What are the limitations of serverless?
Answer: Execution time limits — Lambda has a 15-minute maximum. Memory limits — 10GB maximum. Cold starts when new environments need to be created. No persistent local state — everything has to go to DynamoDB or S3. Limited control over the runtime environment. And vendor lock-in to AWS. Long-running processes like video transcoding or ML training do not fit Lambda. For our DuckDB async queries, we occasionally hit the 15-minute limit on very large queries — we added a timeout handler that returns partial results and a 'query too large' message instead of silently failing.

============================================================

Q117: What is a Bloom Filter and where did you use it in the agent?
Answer: A Bloom filter is a probabilistic data structure that tells you 'definitely not in the set' or 'possibly in the set.' It can have false positives but never false negatives. In our EDR agent, the backend provides a bloom filter URL containing hashes of known-malicious indicators — file hashes, domain names. The agent downloads this compact filter and checks events locally before sending them to the backend. If a file hash is 'possibly' in the filter, the agent sends an enriched alert event. If 'definitely not,' it skips the alert. This reduces network traffic dramatically. Instead of sending every single event to the server for matching, the agent pre-filters locally using the bloom filter. A 10 million entry bloom filter is only about 12 megabytes with a 1 percent false positive rate.

Follow-up 1: Why a bloom filter instead of a full hash list?
Answer: A full list of 10 million SHA-256 hashes would be about 320 megabytes. A bloom filter representing the same set is only about 12 megabytes with a 1 percent false positive rate. The agent runs on customer endpoints with limited resources, so downloading 320 megabytes every policy refresh is not practical. The 1 percent false positive rate is acceptable because false positives just mean sending a few extra events to the backend for verification. False negatives — missing a malicious file — would be unacceptable, and bloom filters guarantee zero false negatives by design.

============================================================

Q118: What is encryption at rest (SSE-S3, SSE-KMS) and how did you use it?
Answer: Encryption at rest means data stored on disk is encrypted — if someone physically steals the storage device, they cannot read the data. AWS S3 offers two main options. SSE-S3 where AWS manages the encryption keys automatically with no configuration needed. And SSE-KMS where you use AWS Key Management Service for key management with rotation, access policies, and audit trails. Our raw events bucket uses SSE-KMS because it contains sensitive security telemetry — we need audit trails showing who decrypted what and when. Customer agent packages use SSE-S3 because the ZIPs are not particularly sensitive — they contain the public binary plus org config, and access is already controlled by presigned URLs.

Follow-up 1: What is the difference between encryption at rest and in transit?
Answer: At rest means data stored on disk — S3 objects, DynamoDB items. In transit means data moving over the network — HTTPS, mTLS. We use both throughout the system. S3 SSE handles encryption at rest. All API communication goes over HTTPS or mTLS for encryption in transit. DynamoDB encrypts at rest by default. Firehose encrypts data in transit to S3 using HTTPS. The agent-to-API Gateway communication uses mTLS, which provides both authentication and encryption in transit. Together, data is never unencrypted at any point in the pipeline.

============================================================

Q119: What is Apollo Client and how did you use it on the frontend?
Answer: Apollo Client is a GraphQL client library for React that handles data fetching, caching, and state management. We use it to communicate with our AppSync backend. There are four key features we leverage. First, the normalized cache — when we fetch an agents list, each agent is cached by its ID. If the user opens an individual agent detail view, Apollo uses the cached data instead of making another network request. Second, optimistic updates — when an admin updates an agent's zone, the UI updates instantly and rolls back if the mutation fails. Third, subscription support — Apollo manages the WebSocket connection to AppSync for real-time async query results. Fourth, automatic loading and error states — each useQuery hook provides loading, error, and data states that map directly to our UI components like skeleton loaders and error boundaries.

Follow-up 1: How does Apollo's normalized cache work?
Answer: Apollo breaks each GraphQL response into individual objects identified by __typename plus id. When the agents list query returns multiple agents, Apollo stores each agent separately in its cache. When a viewAgent query is made for a specific agent, Apollo checks the cache first — if the required fields are already there, it returns the data instantly without a network call. We configure cache policies per query type: the agents list uses cache-and-network, which shows cached data immediately while refreshing in the background. Policy views use network-only because the user always needs the latest version.

============================================================

Q120: What is threat hunting and how does the getHunt/acknowledgeHunt flow work?
Answer: Threat hunting is proactive searching for threats that have not triggered automated alerts. Unlike alerts, which are reactive — the system detects and notifies — hunting is when an analyst says 'I suspect there is lateral movement in our network' and manually searches for indicators. In our platform, the console allows analysts to create hunt tasks that are pushed to agents. The agent calls getHunt during its heartbeat cycle, receives hunt parameters — like 'search for process X connecting to IP range Y' — executes the hunt on the endpoint, and sends results back. Then the agent calls acknowledgeHunt to confirm the task was completed. Think of it as a remote query mechanism — instead of searching the S3 data lake for historical data, the analyst reaches directly into live endpoints for real-time investigation.

Follow-up 1: How is hunting different from async investigation queries?
Answer: Async investigation queries search historical data in the S3 data lake — 'what happened last week?' Hunt searches live endpoints in real time — 'what is happening right now?' Investigation queries are faster for historical analysis because DuckDB queries Parquet files that are already indexed. Hunts are necessary for volatile data that has not been ingested yet, or for real-time forensics — current memory state, active network connections, running processes. Analysts often use both together: they start with an investigation query to identify suspicious patterns in historical data, then create a hunt to investigate the live endpoint in detail.

============================================================

Q121: What is NSIS and how did you use it for Windows agent packaging?
Answer: NSIS — Nullsoft Scriptable Install System — is an open-source tool for creating Windows installers. Our build pipeline uses NSIS to package the OneAgent binary, configuration files like oneagent.json, and the mutual TLS certificates into a self-extracting installer. The NSIS script defines the installation directory, registry entries for the agent service, firewall rules, and the service auto-start configuration. When the user runs the installer, NSIS extracts the payload, writes config files, installs the binary silently, registers the agent as a Windows service, and starts it. We have a dedicated Lambda that generates these NSIS installers per organization because each org's package has unique certificates and API endpoint configuration.

Follow-up 1: Why not use Docker containers for packaging instead of NSIS?
Answer: EDR agents run as native OS services, not containers. They need kernel-level access for process monitoring, file system hooking, and network interception — Docker's isolation model would prevent exactly the kind of deep OS access we need. NSIS produces a traditional Windows installer that registers the agent as a Windows Service through the Service Control Manager. The Linux equivalent is a .deb or .rpm package with a systemd unit file. Containers are great for server workloads, but endpoint security agents need deep OS integration that containers specifically prevent.

============================================================

Q122: What is the difference between WebSocket and polling, and which does your system use?
Answer: Polling means the client repeatedly asks the server 'any updates?' at an interval, like every 5 seconds. WebSocket is a persistent bidirectional connection where the server pushes updates when they happen. Our system uses both, and each for a specific reason. AppSync subscriptions use WebSockets under the hood for real-time async query results — when the DuckDB worker finishes processing, it pushes results through the WebSocket without the frontend asking. But for the agent list and health status, the frontend polls by re-running the GraphQL query every 30 seconds. We chose polling for the agent list because agent data changes slowly — heartbeats come every 60 seconds — and building a push system for 'any agent state changed' across hundreds of organizations would be complex for minimal user experience benefit.

Follow-up 1: What are Server-Sent Events (SSE) and why didn't you use them?
Answer: SSE is a one-directional protocol where the server pushes events to the client over a long-lived HTTP connection. It is simpler than WebSockets but only supports server-to-client communication. We did not use it because AppSync already provides WebSocket-based subscriptions — there is no benefit in adding SSE on top. If we were not using AppSync, SSE would be a good fit for the async query progress updates since those are one-directional. The trade-off compared to WebSockets is that SSE is simpler but limited to text data and one direction.

============================================================

Q123: What is a VPC and did your Lambdas run inside one?
Answer: A VPC — Virtual Private Cloud — is an isolated virtual network in AWS. Resources inside a VPC can communicate with each other but are not accessible from the internet by default. Some of our Lambdas run inside a VPC to access resources like Dragonfly, which is not publicly exposed. The trade-off used to be slower cold starts for VPC Lambdas because of ENI attachment, but AWS improved this with Hyperplane so VPC cold starts are now nearly the same as non-VPC. Lambdas that only access DynamoDB, S3, and SNS — all public AWS endpoints — run outside the VPC for simplicity. Lambdas that need Dragonfly access are inside the VPC with a NAT Gateway for outbound internet access, which they need for Stytch API calls.

Follow-up 1: What is a NAT Gateway?
Answer: A NAT Gateway — Network Address Translation Gateway — allows resources in a private VPC subnet to access the internet without being accessible from the internet. Our VPC Lambdas need outbound internet for Stytch API calls and some AWS service endpoints. Without NAT, a Lambda in a private subnet simply cannot reach the internet. With NAT, outbound requests go through the NAT Gateway which has a public IP. We also use VPC Endpoints for S3 and DynamoDB, which route traffic directly to those services without going through NAT — it is free and faster.

============================================================

Q124: Tell me about the Rule Builder project.
Answer: The Rule Builder is a visual interface I built for security analysts to create, test, and deploy detection rules without writing raw SQL. Think of it this way — in a security product, you need rules that say 'if this suspicious pattern happens, raise an alert.' Before the Rule Builder, creating or modifying these rules required backend engineers to write SQL and deploy changes manually. I built a six-step guided wizard where analysts fill in rule details, build query conditions visually using dropdowns and form fields, configure thresholds and lookup lists, review the generated SQL and JSON, and then save or deploy directly from the console. The wizard generates the correct JSON payload and calls the right REST endpoint — whether it is a create, edit, or clone operation. This made rule management self-service for the security team.

Follow-up 1: How does the Rule Builder connect to the OneAgent platform?
Answer: Rules created in the Rule Builder are stored as structured JSON and compiled into policy documents in S3. When an agent calls getPolicy, it receives these compiled rules as part of its policy configuration. So the Rule Builder is the authoring tool where analysts design and test rules, and OneAgent is the execution layer where those rules actually run on endpoints to detect threats. They share the same DynamoDB tables for policy mappings and the same S3 buckets for policy storage. When an analyst saves and deploys a rule through the Rule Builder, it flows through to the agents on their next policy refresh.

============================================================

Q125: What is the MITRE ATT&CK framework and why is it central to both your projects?
Answer: MITRE ATT&CK is a globally recognized knowledge base that catalogs real-world adversary behaviors into a structured matrix. It organizes attacks into Tactics — the 'why,' meaning the adversary's goal — and Techniques — the 'how,' meaning the specific method they use. In our EDR platform, every detection rule and alert is mapped to MITRE ATT&CK tactics and techniques. The dashboard shows tactic heatmaps through getTacticsOverview and top triggered techniques through getTopMitreAttCkTTPs. In the Rule Builder, analysts tag rules with MITRE IDs during creation, so every rule is connected to the adversary behavior it detects. This shared framework gives security teams a common language. Instead of saying 'we detect suspicious PowerShell,' they say 'we detect T1059.001 PowerShell under TA0002 Execution.' It standardizes communication across the entire security industry.

Follow-up 1: What is a Tactic in MITRE ATT&CK? Name the main ones.
Answer: A Tactic represents the adversary's strategic goal — the reason behind their action. The 14 Enterprise tactics are: TA0001 Initial Access, which is getting into the network. TA0002 Execution, running malicious code. TA0003 Persistence, maintaining access after a reboot. TA0004 Privilege Escalation, gaining higher permissions. TA0005 Defense Evasion, avoiding detection. TA0006 Credential Access, stealing passwords and tokens. TA0007 Discovery, learning about the environment. TA0008 Lateral Movement, moving through the network to other machines. TA0009 Collection, gathering target data. TA0010 Exfiltration, stealing data out of the network. TA0011 Command and Control, communicating with compromised systems. TA0040 Impact, disrupting or destroying systems. TA0042 Resource Development, building attack infrastructure. And TA0043 Reconnaissance, gathering information before the attack. Our dashboard heatmap shows alert counts per tactic so analysts can see which attack stages are most active in their environment.

Follow-up 2: What is a Technique? Give examples relevant to your EDR.
Answer: A Technique is a specific method an adversary uses to achieve a tactic. Here are examples from our actual detection rules. T1059.001 PowerShell under Execution — detecting suspicious PowerShell commands like encoded payloads. T1053.005 Scheduled Task under Persistence — detecting newly created scheduled tasks that could maintain attacker access. T1003.001 LSASS Memory Dump under Credential Access — detecting processes reading LSASS memory to steal credentials. T1071.001 Web Protocols for C2 under Command and Control — detecting beaconing patterns over HTTP or HTTPS. T1486 Data Encrypted for Impact under Impact — detecting ransomware behavior. Each technique has a unique ID, and the rules in our system carry these IDs as MITRE tags so every alert is automatically classified.

Follow-up 3: What are Sub-Techniques?
Answer: Sub-Techniques are more specific variations of a parent technique. For example, T1059 Command and Scripting Interpreter has sub-techniques: T1059.001 for PowerShell, T1059.003 for Windows Command Shell, T1059.004 for Unix Shell, T1059.005 for Visual Basic, and T1059.006 for Python. Our rules map to the sub-technique level whenever possible because it gives more precise detection. Alerting on T1059.001 PowerShell is much more actionable than the generic T1059. The dashboard supports drill-down from technique to sub-technique breakdowns.

============================================================

Q126: How do you map detection rules to MITRE ATT&CK in practice?
Answer: Each detection rule in the policy document includes a 'mitre' field containing the tactic IDs and technique IDs it detects. When the getPolicy Lambda returns the policy JSON to the agent, it runs a transform step that enriches each rule with MITRE metadata — tactic names, technique descriptions. When a rule triggers an alert on the endpoint, the alert event that goes to Kinesis Firehose includes these MITRE tags. The data pipeline preserves them in the Parquet data lake. Then on the dashboard, GraphQL queries like getTacticsOverview group alerts by tactic ID, and getTopMitreAttCkTTPs groups by technique ID. So the MITRE tagging flows end-to-end: from rule creation in the Rule Builder, to policy delivery to the agent, to alert generation, to data lake storage, to dashboard display. Analysts never have to manually classify alerts — every alert carries its MITRE context from creation to display.

Follow-up 1: How does the MITRE heatmap on the dashboard work?
Answer: The getTacticsOverview GraphQL query returns alert counts per tactic for a given time window and organization. The React frontend renders this as a heatmap — each cell represents a tactic, and the color intensity represents how many alerts triggered for that tactic. If the Execution cell is bright red, it means lots of rules related to code execution have been triggering. Analysts click a tactic cell to drill into specific techniques — for example, 'Execution has 500 alerts — 400 are T1059.001 PowerShell and 100 are T1053.005 Scheduled Task.' This layered view helps prioritize investigation — start with the hottest tactic, then drill into the most common techniques within it.

Follow-up 2: What is mitreAttCkTimeline and why does it matter?
Answer: mitreAttCkTimeline shows how tactic triggers change over time — a line chart with time on the x-axis and alert count per tactic on the y-axis. It matters because real attack campaigns progress through tactics in sequence. First you see an Initial Access spike — maybe a phishing email succeeded. Then Execution increases — the attacker runs code. Then Persistence — they install a backdoor. Then Lateral Movement — they spread to other machines. If an analyst sees this progression pattern, it is a strong indicator of an active intrusion, not just isolated noise. The timeline also helps with correlation — 'this Credential Access spike started right after the phishing campaign we detected in Initial Access yesterday.'

Follow-up 3: How does the Rule Builder use MITRE tags?
Answer: In the Rule Builder wizard, when an analyst creates a rule, they tag it with the relevant MITRE tactic and technique in the rule details step. For example, if they are building a rule to detect 'PowerShell downloading files from external URLs,' they tag it with TA0002 Execution and T1059.001 PowerShell. These tags flow into the generated Flink SQL as metadata fields, so when the rule triggers in the streaming pipeline, the resulting alert automatically carries the MITRE context. This also helps with gap analysis — analysts can review the entire rule library, see which MITRE techniques have active rules, and identify gaps like 'we have no rules for TA0010 Exfiltration.' Then they can prioritize creating rules to close those gaps.

============================================================

Q127: What is a MITRE ATT&CK matrix and how would you explain it to a non-security interviewer?
Answer: Think of the ATT&CK matrix as a chessboard for cyber attacks. The columns are Tactics — the stages of an attack, progressing from left to right: getting in, running code, staying hidden, moving through the network, and finally causing damage. The rows within each column are Techniques — the specific moves an attacker can make at each stage. For example, under 'Initial Access,' techniques include phishing emails, exploiting public-facing applications, and compromising supply chains. Our EDR product monitors endpoints and maps every suspicious event to a cell in this matrix. If we see cells lighting up from left to right in sequence — first Initial Access, then Execution, then Persistence — it suggests a coordinated attack progressing through stages. That is when analysts escalate to incident response. The framework turns chaotic security events into a structured narrative that any security professional worldwide understands.

Follow-up 1: How many techniques are in the ATT&CK matrix?
Answer: The Enterprise matrix contains approximately 200 techniques and 400 sub-techniques as of the latest update. No single product covers all of them. Our EDR focuses on endpoint-relevant techniques — Execution, Persistence, Privilege Escalation, Defense Evasion, Credential Access, Discovery, and Lateral Movement — because those behaviors are observable at the endpoint level. Network-level techniques like DNS tunneling for Command and Control require network monitoring tools, not endpoint agents. MITRE regularly updates the matrix as new adversary behaviors are documented by security researchers.

Follow-up 2: What is MITRE ATT&CK coverage and why do customers care?
Answer: Coverage measures how many ATT&CK techniques a security product can detect. Customers compare EDR vendors using MITRE Evaluations — a public test where MITRE simulates real adversary groups like APT29 (Cozy Bear) and measures each vendor's detection performance. Our platform's detection coverage is visible on the dashboard — analysts can see which techniques have active rules and which are gaps. The Rule Builder helps close those gaps quickly. If a MITRE evaluation reveals we miss T1003.001 LSASS dumps, an analyst can immediately create a detection rule for it in the Rule Builder and deploy it to all agents.

============================================================

Q128: Give me a concrete example: how does a single endpoint event flow through MITRE tagging to the analyst's dashboard?
Answer: Let me walk through a real scenario. An attacker runs 'powershell -enc [base64 payload]' on a compromised laptop. Step 1: The EDR agent detects the process creation event — it sees powershell.exe with the -enc flag in the command line. Step 2: The agent's policy has a detection rule tagged with TA0002 Execution and T1059.001 PowerShell that matches this pattern. Step 3: The agent sends the event to Kinesis Firehose with alert metadata including the MITRE tactic 'Execution,' technique 'T1059.001,' and severity 'High.' Step 4: The data pipeline writes this to the Parquet data lake with the MITRE tags preserved. Step 5: On the dashboard, the getTacticsOverview query counts this alert under Execution — the heatmap cell gets darker. Step 6: getTopMitreAttCkTTPs shows T1059.001 trending up. Step 7: The analyst clicks through and sees the specific host, the full command line, the user account, and the timestamp. Step 8: If they want more context, they create an async investigation query through DuckDB to find all other suspicious activity from that host. The MITRE tag was the thread that connected a raw process event all the way to actionable intelligence on the dashboard.

Follow-up 1: What if a single event maps to multiple tactics?
Answer: This happens often in practice. For example, creating a scheduled task with schtasks.exe can be both T1053.005 under Persistence — the attacker is maintaining access — and T1053.005 under Execution — the attacker is running code. Our rules can carry multiple tactic tags. In the data lake, the alert has an array of tactic IDs. Dashboard queries count the alert under each tactic it maps to, so it contributes to both the Persistence and Execution heatmap cells. This is correct per the MITRE framework — the same technique genuinely serves multiple tactical purposes depending on the attacker's intent.

Follow-up 2: How do false positives affect the MITRE dashboard?
Answer: Heavily. If a rule for T1059.001 PowerShell fires on every legitimate admin PowerShell script, the Execution tactic cell glows red with thousands of false positives, drowning out real threats. This is exactly why the Rule Builder's cardinality and lookup list features are so critical. Cardinality adds thresholds — 'alert only if more than 10 encoded PowerShell commands happen in 5 minutes.' Lookup lists let you exclude known-good scripts — 'if powershell.exe is in the admin_scripts list, do not alert.' Tuning rules through the Rule Builder directly cleans up the MITRE dashboard. Fewer false positives means the heatmap accurately reflects real adversary activity, which means analysts trust the data and respond faster to actual threats.

============================================================

Q129: What is the Rule Builder product and why does it exist?
Answer: The Rule Builder is a visual query-builder and lookup-list manager for the BluSapphire One Platform. It lets security analysts and admins define alert/detection rules through a guided UI — picking data sources, building conditions with AND/OR logic, setting cardinality and observables, configuring notifications — instead of writing raw SQL or editing JSON by hand. This reduces mistakes, speeds up rule creation, and keeps detection logic consistent across the organization.

Follow-up 1: Who are the primary users?
Answer: Three roles from the Keycloak JWT: serviceprovideradmin (full CRUD on rules and lookup lists, can create SP rules), serviceprovideranalyst (can create custom rules, limited lookup list access), and clientadmin (manages rules for their own organization). Role determines which rule types they can create and what lookup list operations they can perform.

Follow-up 2: How does it differ from writing raw detection queries?
Answer: The UI enforces a structured workflow — six steps with validation gates — so analysts can't deploy a rule missing required fields. It generates Flink SQL automatically via a backend service, shows a review step with copyable JSON and SQL, and supports Save vs Save & Deploy. This eliminates syntax errors and gives a clear audit trail of what was deployed.

============================================================

Q130: Walk me through the end-to-end architecture of Rule Builder.
Answer: The frontend is a React 18 + Vite + TypeScript SPA. Auth is handled by Keycloak (check-sso mode) with JWT tokens. State lives in a React Context (QueryBuilderContext). The app talks to two types of backends: REST APIs on the One Platform (rule CRUD, categories, FOI, org lists, plugins, templates, validation) and two GraphQL endpoints — Discover (for getColumns/table schemas) and Rule Builder FastAPI (for generateFlinkQuery and Lookup List CRUD). A token exchange flow converts the Keycloak access_token into an api_token for REST calls. The dev proxy routes /restapi to oneplatform.blusapphire.com.

Follow-up 1: Why two separate GraphQL endpoints?
Answer: Separation of concerns. The Discover endpoint serves table schema metadata (column names, types) used across multiple platform features — not just Rule Builder. The Rule Builder FastAPI endpoint handles rule-specific operations: Flink query generation and Lookup List CRUD. Apollo Client uses a split link to route operations to the correct endpoint based on the operation name.

Follow-up 2: Why REST + GraphQL instead of just one?
Answer: The REST APIs are the existing One Platform backend — rule lifecycle, categories, org management, notification plugins. These were already built before Rule Builder. The GraphQL endpoints were added specifically for schema discovery and query generation because they benefit from GraphQL's typed schema and the ability to request exactly the fields needed. It's a pragmatic mix, not a greenfield choice.

============================================================

Q131: What is Keycloak and why is it used in Rule Builder?
Answer: Keycloak is an open-source Identity and Access Management (IAM) solution that provides Single Sign-On (SSO), user federation, and role-based access control. In Rule Builder, it runs in 'check-sso' mode — the app silently checks if the user already has a valid session. The JWT token contains user roles (serviceprovideradmin, serviceprovideranalyst, clientadmin) that the frontend reads via jwt-decode to control UI visibility — which rule types to show, which lookup list operations to enable.

Follow-up 1: What is check-sso mode?
Answer: check-sso is a Keycloak initialization mode where the adapter silently checks for an existing SSO session without redirecting to a login page. If a session exists, the token is loaded; if not, the app can handle it gracefully. This is different from 'login-required' mode which always redirects to the Keycloak login page. Rule Builder uses check-sso because users are expected to already be logged into the One Platform.

Follow-up 2: Explain the token exchange flow.
Answer: After Keycloak auth, the frontend has an access_token. For REST API calls, it POSTs this access_token to the token exchange endpoint (VITE_REACT_API_BASE_URL_KEYCLOAK) which returns an api_token. REST calls then send both the api_token in the request body AND the original Keycloak token in the Authorization header. This two-token approach lets the backend validate the user's identity (Keycloak token) while using the api_token for platform-specific authorization.

Follow-up 3: How is token refresh handled?
Answer: The Keycloak adapter automatically refreshes tokens when they're about to expire (configured around 30 seconds before expiry). The frontend checks token validity before API calls and triggers a refresh if needed. This prevents mid-workflow auth failures — an analyst building a complex rule won't lose their work because a token expired.

============================================================

Q132: What is jwt-decode and how is it used?
Answer: jwt-decode is a lightweight library that decodes JSON Web Tokens without verifying the signature (verification happens server-side). In Rule Builder, it extracts the user's roles from the Keycloak JWT payload. The decoded roles (serviceprovideradmin, serviceprovideranalyst, clientadmin) determine UI behavior — service provider admins see the SP rule type option, get full lookup list CRUD, and can access org-management features that other roles cannot.

Follow-up 1: Is it safe to decode JWT on the client without verification?
Answer: Yes, because the client isn't making authorization decisions — it's only using the decoded roles for UI rendering (showing/hiding buttons, routing options). All actual authorization happens server-side when the token is sent with API calls. The backend verifies the signature and checks roles before executing any operation. Client-side decoding is purely a UX convenience.

============================================================

Q133: What is QueryBuilderContext and why use React Context instead of Redux?
Answer: QueryBuilderContext is a React Context that holds the entire rule-builder state: rule details (name, description, priority, type), data source (category, query groups/conditions), cardinality settings, observables, and notification config. It's used instead of Redux because the state is scoped to a single workflow — the six-step rule wizard. There's no need for global state management across unrelated features. A Redux store exists in the codebase but isn't used by Rule Builder screens, likely a legacy artifact from the broader platform.

Follow-up 1: What does the context state shape look like?
Answer: It includes: ruleDetails (name, description, priority, ruleType), dataSource (selectedCategory, queryGroups with nested AND/OR conditions, each condition having field/operator/value), cardinality (enabled, groupByFields, timespan, operator, threshold), observables (selected field list), forward (enabled, plugin, template, params), and metadata like isDataReady flags for each section.

Follow-up 2: How does section gating work?
Answer: Each wizard section checks isDataReady for previous sections. For example, you can't proceed to Cardinality until Data Source has a valid category and at least one condition. The context exposes computed flags (isRuleDetailsReady, isDataSourceReady, etc.) that the sidebar reads to enable/disable navigation. This prevents incomplete rules from reaching the Review step.

============================================================

Q134: What is Apollo Client and how is it configured in Rule Builder?
Answer: Apollo Client is a GraphQL client library that manages data fetching, caching, and state for GraphQL operations. In Rule Builder, it's configured with a split link — an Apollo Link that routes operations to different endpoints based on the operation name. Operations like getColumns go to the Discover GraphQL endpoint, while generateFlinkQuery and lookup list mutations go to the Rule Builder FastAPI endpoint. Auth is handled via an auth link that attaches the Bearer token to every request.

Follow-up 1: What is a split link in Apollo?
Answer: A split link is an Apollo Link that acts as a router — it takes a boolean function and two links. If the function returns true, the operation goes to one link; if false, it goes to the other. In Rule Builder, the split checks the operation name: if it's 'getColumns', it routes to the Discover HTTP link; otherwise, it routes to the Rule Builder FastAPI HTTP link. This lets a single Apollo Client instance talk to multiple GraphQL backends seamlessly.

Follow-up 2: What is the normalized cache in Apollo?
Answer: Apollo Client stores query results in a normalized, in-memory cache keyed by object type and ID. When you fetch columns for a table, the result is cached so subsequent visits to the same data source don't trigger a network request. This makes the wizard feel snappy when analysts go back and forth between steps. Cache can be manually updated or evicted when data changes.

============================================================

Q135: What is Axios and how is it used for REST calls?
Answer: Axios is an HTTP client library used for all REST API calls to the One Platform backend. In Rule Builder, it handles rule CRUD (create_custom_rule, update_base_rule, etc.), category listing (srarules_category_list_get), default FOI retrieval (get_default_foi), organization listing, notification plugin/template fetching, and plugin validation. Each call includes the api_token (from token exchange) in the body and the Keycloak token in the Authorization header.

Follow-up 1: Why Axios instead of fetch?
Answer: Axios provides automatic JSON parsing, request/response interceptors (useful for attaching auth headers globally), timeout configuration, and better error handling with status codes. The existing One Platform codebase standardized on Axios, so Rule Builder follows the same pattern for consistency. Interceptors handle token attachment so individual API functions don't need to manage auth headers manually.

============================================================

Q136: What does the getColumns GraphQL query do?
Answer: getColumns queries the Discover GraphQL endpoint to fetch the schema of a selected data source table — column names and their data types. It takes parameters like table name, organization code, and optional filters. The returned columns populate the field dropdowns in the query builder so analysts pick from actual table fields rather than typing column names manually. This prevents typos and ensures conditions reference valid fields.

Follow-up 1: Why is this a GraphQL query instead of REST?
Answer: The Discover service is a shared schema-metadata service used across multiple platform features. GraphQL is a natural fit because different consumers need different subsets of column metadata — Rule Builder needs names and types, other tools might need statistics or sample values. GraphQL lets each consumer request exactly what it needs without the backend maintaining multiple REST endpoints for different shapes.

============================================================

Q137: What does generateFlinkQuery do and why is it important?
Answer: generateFlinkQuery is a GraphQL mutation on the Rule Builder FastAPI endpoint. It takes an org code and a base64-encoded JSON of the rule definition (query groups, conditions, cardinality, etc.) and returns three things: responsedata (the generated Flink SQL as base64), wherecondition (the WHERE clause), and cardinality info. This is critical because it translates the visual query builder's structured JSON into executable Flink SQL that the detection engine runs. It's called during the Review step (so analysts can preview the SQL) and during Save (the generated SQL is included in the save payload).

Follow-up 1: What is Apache Flink and why Flink SQL?
Answer: Apache Flink is a distributed stream-processing framework. BluSapphire uses it as the detection engine — rules are expressed as Flink SQL queries that continuously process incoming event streams (endpoint telemetry) and trigger alerts when conditions match. The Rule Builder generates Flink SQL because that's the execution format the backend detection pipeline understands. Analysts see SQL in the Review tab but never need to write it.

Follow-up 2: Why base64 encode the JSON?
Answer: Base64 encoding ensures the complex nested JSON (with special characters, quotes, nested arrays of conditions) is safely transported as a single string parameter in the GraphQL mutation without escaping issues. The backend decodes it, processes the rule structure, and returns the generated SQL also base64-encoded for the same safety reasons.

============================================================

Q138: What REST endpoints does Rule Builder use and what does each do?
Answer: Key REST endpoints: getCategoryList (srarules_category_list_get) fetches available data source categories/tables. getDefaultFOI (get_default_foi) returns default Fields of Interest for a category — used to populate Cardinality group-by and Observable field options. get_rule_json loads an existing rule for edit/clone. create_custom_rule and create_sp_rule save new rules. update_base_rule, update_custom_rule, update_sp_rule handle edits based on rule parent type. user_org_list_get fetches organizations for notifications. forward_rule_plugins_list_get and ams_templates_list_get load notification plugins and templates. validate_plugin_data validates notification config before save.

Follow-up 1: What is FOI (Fields of Interest)?
Answer: FOI is a concept in the detection platform — each data source category has a default set of 'interesting' fields that analysts typically want to group by or observe. For example, a network-events category might have FOI like source_ip, dest_ip, protocol. The Rule Builder fetches these via getDefaultFOI and uses them to pre-populate the Cardinality group-by dropdowns and Observable field checkboxes, so analysts start with sensible defaults instead of scrolling through hundreds of raw columns.

Follow-up 2: How does the save endpoint selection work?
Answer: The Review component checks three things from URL params and context: whether it's a new rule or edit (type param), the rule parent (custom, baserule, sp), and the user's role. New custom rules go to create_custom_rule, new SP rules to create_sp_rule. Edits route to update_base_rule, update_custom_rule, or update_sp_rule depending on the parent param. Clone behaves like a new rule (create endpoint) but pre-fills all fields from the source rule.

============================================================

Q139: Explain the six-step rule wizard flow.
Answer: Step 1 — Rule Details: name, description, priority (1-5), rule type (Custom or SP based on role). Step 2 — Data Source: select category/table, build query with visual query builder (nested AND/OR groups, conditions with field/operator/value). Step 3 — Cardinality: optional, enable group-by fields with timespan, operator (>=, <=, ==), and threshold value. Step 4 — Observables: select which fields to include as alert observables. Step 5 — Notifications: enable forwarding, select plugin (email, Slack, webhook, etc.), template, and configure params with validation. Step 6 — Review: summary table, copyable JSON, copyable SQL (from generateFlinkQuery), Save or Save & Deploy buttons.

Follow-up 1: Can you skip steps?
Answer: No. The sidebar disables later steps until earlier ones are complete. Rule Details (name required) gates everything. Data Source (category + at least one condition) gates Cardinality onward. Cardinality and Observables are optional but you must pass through them. Notifications is optional. Review requires all mandatory data to be ready. This prevents analysts from deploying incomplete rules.

Follow-up 2: What happens with Save vs Save & Deploy?
Answer: Save creates/updates the rule in a disabled state — it's stored but not actively processing events. Save & Deploy creates/updates AND enables the rule immediately, so the Flink job starts processing the stream. This distinction lets analysts draft rules, get them reviewed by a senior, and only deploy when ready. The deploy flag is sent as a boolean in the REST payload.

============================================================

Q140: How does the visual query builder work?
Answer: The query builder renders nested groups connected by AND/OR logic. Each group contains conditions (field + operator + value). Analysts select a field from a dropdown (populated by getColumns), pick an operator (Equal, Not Equal, IN, Not IN, Contains, Not Contains, isInPredefinedList, REGEXP, Greater Than, Less Than, etc.), and enter a value. Groups can be nested — e.g., (A AND B) OR (C AND D). The entire structure is stored as a JSON tree in QueryBuilderContext and sent to generateFlinkQuery for SQL conversion.

Follow-up 1: What is isInPredefinedList and how does it work?
Answer: isInPredefinedList is a special operator that checks if a field's value exists in a Lookup List. When an analyst selects this operator, the value dropdown is populated by fetching all lookup list names via the getAllRuleData GraphQL query. Instead of typing individual values, the analyst picks a list name (e.g., 'known_malicious_ips'). At runtime, the Flink query checks the field value against that list. This is powerful for maintaining large whitelists/blacklists without editing individual rules.

Follow-up 2: How are nested groups represented in state?
Answer: The state is a recursive tree: each group has a 'combinator' (AND/OR), an array of 'rules' (conditions with field/operator/value), and an array of 'groups' (child groups with the same structure). The UI renders this recursively — each group component can contain rule rows and nested group components. This maps directly to SQL: each group becomes a parenthesized expression joined by AND/OR.

============================================================

Q141: What operators are available in the query builder and why?
Answer: Equal/Not Equal for exact matches. IN/Not IN for multi-value matching (e.g., port IN [80, 443, 8080]). Contains/Not Contains for substring matching on string fields. REGEXP for pattern matching (e.g., matching URL patterns). Greater Than/Less Than for numeric comparisons. isInPredefinedList for lookup-list-based matching. Each operator maps to a specific Flink SQL construct — the generateFlinkQuery backend knows how to translate each operator type into valid SQL syntax.

Follow-up 1: How does the UI adapt based on operator selection?
Answer: The value input changes based on the operator: Equal/Not Equal shows a single text input. IN/Not IN shows a multi-value input (comma-separated or chips). isInPredefinedList shows a dropdown of lookup list names. REGEXP shows a text input with pattern hint. Greater Than/Less Than show a numeric input. Contains shows a text input. This dynamic rendering prevents invalid input — you can't accidentally type a string for a numeric comparison.

============================================================

Q142: What is cardinality in the context of Rule Builder?
Answer: Cardinality is an optional rule feature that adds aggregation logic. Instead of alerting on every single event that matches the query, cardinality groups events by specified fields over a time window and only alerts when the count meets a threshold. For example: 'Alert when the same source_ip hits more than 100 unique dest_ports in 5 minutes.' The group-by fields come from the FOI defaults, and the analyst sets the timespan, comparison operator (>=, <=, ==), and threshold value.

Follow-up 1: How does cardinality translate to Flink SQL?
Answer: The generateFlinkQuery backend adds a GROUP BY clause with the selected fields, a TUMBLE window function for the timespan, and a HAVING clause with the count threshold. For example: SELECT source_ip, COUNT(DISTINCT dest_port) as cnt FROM events WHERE [conditions] GROUP BY source_ip, TUMBLE(event_time, INTERVAL '5' MINUTE) HAVING cnt >= 100. This turns a simple filter rule into a statistical detection rule.

Follow-up 2: Why is cardinality important for security detection?
Answer: Many attacks aren't detectable from single events — port scans, brute force, data exfiltration show as patterns over time. Cardinality lets analysts define these time-based thresholds: '50 failed logins in 10 minutes from the same IP' or '1000 DNS queries to the same domain in 1 minute.' Without cardinality, analysts would need to write complex windowed SQL queries manually, which is error-prone and slow to iterate.

============================================================

Q143: What is the Lookup List feature and how does it work?
Answer: Lookup List is the second tab in Rule Builder. It manages named lists of values (e.g., 'known_safe_ips', 'malicious_domains') that rules can reference via the isInPredefinedList operator. The UI shows two DataGrids side by side — the left grid lists all lookup list names for the selected org, and the right grid shows the values in the selected list. Users can add/edit/delete lists and values, upload values via CSV, and copy list names. All data flows through GraphQL mutations on the Rule Builder FastAPI endpoint.

Follow-up 1: How does CSV upload work?
Answer: The upload dialog accepts a CSV file, parses it client-side to extract values (one per row or comma-separated), and sends them as an array to the addRuleBulderData or editRuleData GraphQL mutation. This lets analysts import thousands of indicators (IPs, domains, hashes) from threat intelligence feeds without manually entering each one. The UI shows a preview before confirming.

Follow-up 2: How does role-based access work for Lookup Lists?
Answer: The Keycloak JWT role controls visibility: serviceprovideradmin gets full CRUD — add lists, upload CSV, edit values, delete lists. serviceprovideranalyst can view lists and use them in rules but has limited write access. clientadmin can manage lists for their own organization. The frontend checks the decoded role and conditionally renders add/upload/delete buttons. Backend enforces the same rules so even if someone bypasses the UI, the mutation fails.

============================================================

Q144: How does URL-driven behavior work in Rule Builder?
Answer: Rule Builder is a single-page app with no route paths — navigation is tab-based (Rule Builder | Lookup List) and sidebar-based (wizard steps). However, it reads URL search params (base64-encoded): 'oc' (organization code), 'rule_id', 'type' (edit/clone), and 'parent' (custom/baserule/sp). These are parsed in MainContent via useLocation(). New rule: only 'oc' present, context initialized with defaults. Edit: fetches the rule via get_rule_json, decodes the base64 rule JSON, and populates all context fields. Clone: same as edit but assigns a new ID and appends ' - Clone' to the name.

Follow-up 1: Why base64 encode URL params?
Answer: The One Platform links to Rule Builder from various places — alert detail pages, rule management tables. Base64 encoding prevents special characters in org codes, rule IDs, or parent values from breaking URL parsing. It also provides a minimal layer of obscurity so end users don't casually modify URL params, though it's not a security measure.

Follow-up 2: What happens when you clone a rule?
Answer: Clone loads the full rule JSON (same as edit), then modifies two things: generates a new unique ID and appends ' - Clone' to the rule name. All other fields — query, cardinality, observables, notifications — are preserved. The save path uses create endpoints (not update) so the clone becomes an independent rule. This lets analysts duplicate a working rule and tweak it for a variation, like adjusting thresholds for a different org.

============================================================

Q145: Describe the complete data flow when saving a rule.
Answer: 1) Review component reads all state from QueryBuilderContext. 2) Builds a single JSON object: ruleDetails + dataSource (with typed query tree) + cardinality + observables + forward (notifications). 3) Base64-encodes this JSON and calls generateFlinkQuery GraphQL mutation with orgcode and the encoded JSON. 4) Backend returns: responsedata (Flink SQL base64), wherecondition, cardinality info. 5) Frontend constructs the REST payload: api_token, query_type 'sql', body (Flink base64), where_condition, rule_category, forward config, deploy flag, enabled flag, rule_name, json_rule (original base64), is_cardinality flag. 6) Selects the correct REST endpoint based on new/edit + parent type. 7) POSTs to the One Platform REST API. 8) Shows success/error toast.

Follow-up 1: What if generateFlinkQuery fails?
Answer: The Review component shows an error toast and prevents save. The analyst can go back to Data Source to fix conditions. Common failure reasons: invalid field names (stale column data), incompatible operator-value combinations, or malformed nested groups. The error message from the backend is displayed so the analyst knows what to fix.

============================================================

Q146: How does the notification system work in Rule Builder?
Answer: Step 5 (Notifications) lets analysts configure alert forwarding — when a rule triggers, where should the notification go?
